\mainmatter
\chapter{How to Solve Calculus Word Problems}
\begin{strategybox}
A calculus word problem has two major phases: \textbf{build the mathematical model} and then
\textbf{solve the model with the appropriate mathematical tools}. The discipline in this workbook is to
make the modeling phase visible rather than jumping directly to symbolic manipulation.
\end{strategybox}
\section{Nine-step workflow}
\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Read for meaning.} Identify the situation, given data, unknown quantity, units, and restrictions.
\item \textbf{Draw or organize the situation.} Use a diagram, coordinate system, table, timeline, force diagram, or compartment sketch when it clarifies relationships.
\item \textbf{Define variables.} State every variable and its units. Distinguish independent variables, dependent variables, parameters, and constants.
\item \textbf{Translate words into mathematical relationships.} Write equations, inequalities, physical laws, constraints, initial conditions, or boundary conditions before doing calculus.
\item \textbf{Reduce the model.} Use constraints to eliminate unnecessary variables when possible.
\item \textbf{Identify the calculus technique.} Explain why a derivative, integral, ODE, optimization method, vector operation, or numerical method is appropriate.
\item \textbf{Execute the mathematics.} Carry out the calculus and algebra, keeping exact quantities as long as useful.
\item \textbf{Validate.} Check units, domain, sign, magnitude, feasibility, endpoints, initial conditions, and residuals.
\item \textbf{Interpret.} End with a sentence that answers the original applied question in context.
\end{enumerate}
\section{How to use the workbook}
First attempt each problem without looking at a worked solution. If stuck, identify the exact modeling step that failed, review only that step, then restart the problem. Mixed reviews intentionally omit the technique label so that method selection becomes part of the task.
\section{Difficulty levels}
\begin{description}
\item[Foundation (F)] The model is nearly explicit; emphasis is on recognizing the calculus idea.
\item[Standard (S)] The learner must build the model and carry out several linked steps.
\item[Advanced (A)] The problem combines nontrivial modeling, several mathematical steps, or a computational extension.
\end{description}

\part{Modeling Foundations}
\chapter{Population, cell-phone subscribers, GDP, apartment rental and other baseline trend models}\label{app:A001}
\textbf{Application ID:} \pid{A001}\quad \textbf{Source locus:} P.1–P.3\quad \textbf{Source pages:} 9, 12, 16, 18
\section*{Application Context}
This family focuses on translating observations and verbal relationships into functions that can be evaluated, compared, and differentiated. The canonical application family is \emph{Population, cell-phone subscribers, GDP, apartment rental and other baseline trend models}.
\section*{Calculus Toolkit}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}lX@{}}\toprule
Primary technique & Functions, graphs, linear models and rates \\
Prerequisites & algebra, functions, units, graphs \\
Numerical extension & Ch. 15 (modeling of data); Ch. 3 when interpolation is used \\
Analytical/numerical class & NUMERICAL-OPTIONAL \\
\bottomrule\end{tabularx}
\section*{Modeling Questions to Ask}\begin{itemize}
\item Which quantity is the independent variable?
\item What output is being modeled?
\item What units belong to the model parameters?
\item Over what domain is the model credible?
\end{itemize}
\begin{errorbox}Do not start differentiating before the variables, units, and functional relationship are explicit. Check the domain before extrapolating a fitted model.\end{errorbox}
\section*{Problems}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-001-F-01} — Foundation}
\begin{problembox}
A monitoring team studying population, cell-phone subscribers, gdp, apartment rental and other baseline trend models records a value of 22 units at time 0 and 38 units at time 4. Assume a linear model over the next few time units. Construct the model and predict the value at time 7.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which quantity is the independent variable?
\item What output is being modeled?
\item What units belong to the model parameters?
\item Over what domain is the model credible?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Two observations define a short-range linear model; the requested output is the modeled value at time 7.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par Let $t$ be time and let $Q(t)$ be the measured quantity.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $Q(t)=Q(0)+mt$ with $m=(38-22)/(4-0)=4$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par This is a modeling-foundation problem; the slope is the constant rate of change.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $Q(t)=22+4t$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $Q(7)=22+4(7)=50$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The predicted value lies beyond the two observations, so it is an extrapolation; the units remain those of the measured quantity.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The linear model predicts approximately $50$ units at time 7.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The essential step is translating two observations into a rate and intercept before evaluating the model.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-001-S-01} — Standard}
\begin{problembox}
For a short planning horizon, a quantity associated with population, cell-phone subscribers, gdp, apartment rental and other baseline trend models is modeled by $Q(t)=53e^{0.045t}$, where $t$ is measured in years. Find the instantaneous growth rate at $t=5$ and interpret it.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which quantity is the independent variable?
\item What output is being modeled?
\item What units belong to the model parameters?
\item Over what domain is the model credible?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par The model is given; the requested quantity is the instantaneous rate of change.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $Q(t)$ is the modeled quantity and $t$ is time in years.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $Q(t)=53e^{0.045t}$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Differentiate because an instantaneous rate is requested.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $Q'(t)=53(0.045)e^{0.045t}=2.385e^{0.045t}$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $Q'(5)\approx 2.987$ units/year.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The derivative is positive, consistent with an increasing exponential model.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par At $t=5$, the quantity is increasing at approximately $2.987$ units per year.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The wording “instantaneous growth rate” identifies the derivative as the required operation.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-001-A-01} — Advanced}
\begin{problembox}
Measurements related to population, cell-phone subscribers, gdp, apartment rental and other baseline trend models are 32 units initially and 47.4 units after 6 time units. Assume $Q(t)=Q_0e^{rt}$. Estimate $r$, then determine when the model first reaches 57.6 units.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which quantity is the independent variable?
\item What output is being modeled?
\item What units belong to the model parameters?
\item Over what domain is the model credible?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Use two observations to identify an exponential model, then invert the model to find a threshold time.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $Q(t)$ is the measured quantity; $Q_0$ is its initial value; $r$ is the continuous growth rate.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $47.4=32e^{6r}$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par The modeling step uses logarithms to solve for an exponential parameter; the resulting model is then inverted.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $r=\frac1{6}\ln(47.4/32)\approx 0.06534$. For the threshold, $t=\ln(57.6/32)/r$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $t\approx 8.996$ time units.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par $Q(8.996)\approx 57.6$, so the model reproduces the requested threshold.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The fitted continuous rate is approximately $6.53\%$ per time unit, and the threshold is reached after about $9.00$ time units.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The key modeling decision is recognizing that two positive observations determine the exponential rate once the initial value is known.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\begin{reflectionbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which sentence or datum in these problems supplied the main mathematical constraint?
\item Which assumptions were essential to the model, and how would the model change if one were removed?
\item When would an analytical solution become inconvenient enough to justify a numerical method?
\end{enumerate}\end{reflectionbox}

\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par This is a modeling-foundation problem; the slope is the constant rate of change.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $Q(t)=24+5t$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $Q(7)=24+5(7)=59$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The predicted value lies beyond the two observations, so it is an extrapolation; the units remain those of the measured quantity.\par\medskip

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-002-S-01} — Standard}
\begin{problembox}
For a short planning horizon, a quantity associated with carbon-dioxide concentration and environmental trend models is modeled by $Q(t)=56e^{0.050t}$, where $t$ is measured in years. Find the instantaneous growth rate at $t=5$ and interpret it.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which quantity is the independent variable?
\item What output is being modeled?
\item What units belong to the model parameters?
\item Over what domain is the model credible?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par The model is given; the requested quantity is the instantaneous rate of change.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $Q(t)$ is the modeled quantity and $t$ is time in years.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $Q(t)=56e^{0.050t}$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Differentiate because an instantaneous rate is requested.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $Q'(t)=56(0.050)e^{0.050t}=2.800e^{0.050t}$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $Q'(5)\approx 3.595$ units/year.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The derivative is positive, consistent with an increasing exponential model.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par At $t=5$, the quantity is increasing at approximately $3.595$ units per year.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The wording “instantaneous growth rate” identifies the derivative as the required operation.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-002-A-01} — Advanced}
\begin{problembox}
Measurements related to carbon-dioxide concentration and environmental trend models are 34 units initially and 51.3 units after 6 time units. Assume $Q(t)=Q_0e^{rt}$. Estimate $r$, then determine when the model first reaches 61.2 units.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which quantity is the independent variable?
\item What output is being modeled?
\item What units belong to the model parameters?
\item Over what domain is the model credible?

\textbf{Construct the model}\par $51.3=34e^{6r}$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par The modeling step uses logarithms to solve for an exponential parameter; the resulting model is then inverted.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $r=\frac1{6}\ln(51.3/34)\approx 0.06868$. For the threshold, $t=\ln(61.2/34)/r$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $t\approx 8.558$ time units.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par $Q(8.558)\approx 61.2$, so the model reproduces the requested threshold.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The fitted continuous rate is approximately $6.87\%$ per time unit, and the threshold is reached after about $8.56$ time units.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The key modeling decision is recognizing that two positive observations determine the exponential rate once the initial value is known.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}

\item Which sentence or datum in these problems supplied the main mathematical constraint?
\item Which assumptions were essential to the model, and how would the model change if one were removed?

\chapter{Ferris-wheel height, angular position and periodic geometry}\label{app:A004}
\textbf{Application ID:} \pid{A004}\quad \textbf{Source locus:} P.4; 12\quad \textbf{Source pages:} 40, 870
\section*{Application Context}
This family focuses on translating observations and verbal relationships into functions that can be evaluated, compared, and differentiated. The canonical application family is \emph{Ferris-wheel height, angular position and periodic geometry}.
\section*{Calculus Toolkit}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}lX@{}}\toprule
Primary technique & Trigonometric functions and parametric motion \\
Prerequisites & algebra, functions, units, graphs \\
Numerical extension & Ch. 15 (modeling of data); Ch. 3 when interpolation is used \\
Analytical/numerical class & ANALYTICAL \\
\bottomrule\end{tabularx}
\section*{Modeling Questions to Ask}\begin{itemize}
\item Which quantity is the independent variable?
\item What output is being modeled?
\item What units belong to the model parameters?
\item Over what domain is the model credible?
\end{itemize}
\begin{errorbox}Do not start differentiating before the variables, units, and functional relationship are explicit. Check the domain before extrapolating a fitted model.\end{errorbox}
\section*{Problems}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-004-F-01} — Foundation}
\begin{problembox}
A monitoring team studying ferris-wheel height, angular position and periodic geometry records a value of 28 units at time 0 and 40 units at time 4. Assume a linear model over the next few time units. Construct the model and predict the value at time 7.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which quantity is the independent variable?
\item What output is being modeled?
\item What units belong to the model parameters?
\item Over what domain is the model credible?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Two observations define a short-range linear model; the requested output is the modeled value at time 7.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par Let $t$ be time and let $Q(t)$ be the measured quantity.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $Q(t)=Q(0)+mt$ with $m=(40-28)/(4-0)=3$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par This is a modeling-foundation problem; the slope is the constant rate of change.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $Q(t)=28+3t$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $Q(7)=28+3(7)=49$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The predicted value lies beyond the two observations, so it is an extrapolation; the units remain those of the measured quantity.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The linear model predicts approximately $49$ units at time 7.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The essential step is translating two observations into a rate and intercept before evaluating the model.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-004-S-01} — Standard}
\begin{problembox}
For a short planning horizon, a quantity associated with ferris-wheel height, angular position and periodic geometry is modeled by $Q(t)=62e^{0.040t}$, where $t$ is measured in years. Find the instantaneous growth rate at $t=5$ and interpret it.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which quantity is the independent variable?
\item What output is being modeled?
\item What units belong to the model parameters?
\item Over what domain is the model credible?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par The model is given; the requested quantity is the instantaneous rate of change.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $Q(t)$ is the modeled quantity and $t$ is time in years.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $Q(t)=62e^{0.040t}$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Differentiate because an instantaneous rate is requested.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $Q'(t)=62(0.040)e^{0.040t}=2.480e^{0.040t}$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $Q'(5)\approx 3.029$ units/year.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The derivative is positive, consistent with an increasing exponential model.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par At $t=5$, the quantity is increasing at approximately $3.029$ units per year.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The wording “instantaneous growth rate” identifies the derivative as the required operation.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-004-A-01} — Advanced}
\begin{problembox}
Measurements related to ferris-wheel height, angular position and periodic geometry are 38 units initially and 56.2 units after 6 time units. Assume $Q(t)=Q_0e^{rt}$. Estimate $r$, then determine when the model first reaches 68.4 units.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which quantity is the independent variable?
\item What output is being modeled?
\item What units belong to the model parameters?
\item Over what domain is the model credible?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Use two observations to identify an exponential model, then invert the model to find a threshold time.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $Q(t)$ is the measured quantity; $Q_0$ is its initial value; $r$ is the continuous growth rate.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $56.2=38e^{6r}$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par The modeling step uses logarithms to solve for an exponential parameter; the resulting model is then inverted.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $r=\frac1{6}\ln(56.2/38)\approx 0.06534$. For the threshold, $t=\ln(68.4/38)/r$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $t\approx 8.996$ time units.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par $Q(8.996)\approx 68.4$, so the model reproduces the requested threshold.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The fitted continuous rate is approximately $6.53\%$ per time unit, and the threshold is reached after about $9.00$ time units.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The key modeling decision is recognizing that two positive observations determine the exponential rate once the initial value is known.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\begin{reflectionbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which sentence or datum in these problems supplied the main mathematical constraint?
\item Which assumptions were essential to the model, and how would the model change if one were removed?
\item When would an analytical solution become inconvenient enough to justify a numerical method?
\end{enumerate}\end{reflectionbox}
\chapter{Automobile aerodynamics, thermostat and ripple models}\label{app:A005}
\textbf{Application ID:} \pid{A005}\quad \textbf{Source locus:} P.3; 2.6\quad \textbf{Source pages:} 29–30, 153
\section*{Application Context}
This family focuses on translating observations and verbal relationships into functions that can be evaluated, compared, and differentiated. The canonical application family is \emph{Automobile aerodynamics, thermostat and ripple models}.
\section*{Calculus Toolkit}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}lX@{}}\toprule
Primary technique & Functional dependence and geometry \\
Prerequisites & algebra, functions, units, graphs \\
Numerical extension & Ch. 15 (modeling of data); Ch. 3 when interpolation is used \\
Analytical/numerical class & NUMERICAL-OPTIONAL \\
\bottomrule\end{tabularx}
\section*{Modeling Questions to Ask}\begin{itemize}
\item Which quantity is the independent variable?
\item What output is being modeled?
\item What units belong to the model parameters?
\item Over what domain is the model credible?
\end{itemize}
\begin{errorbox}Do not start differentiating before the variables, units, and functional relationship are explicit. Check the domain before extrapolating a fitted model.\end{errorbox}
\section*{Problems}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-005-F-01} — Foundation}
\begin{problembox}
A monitoring team studying automobile aerodynamics, thermostat and ripple models records a value of 30 units at time 0 and 46 units at time 4. Assume a linear model over the next few time units. Construct the model and predict the value at time 7.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which quantity is the independent variable?
\item What output is being modeled?
\item What units belong to the model parameters?
\item Over what domain is the model credible?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Two observations define a short-range linear model; the requested output is the modeled value at time 7.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par Let $t$ be time and let $Q(t)$ be the measured quantity.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $Q(t)=Q(0)+mt$ with $m=(46-30)/(4-0)=4$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par This is a modeling-foundation problem; the slope is the constant rate of change.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $Q(t)=30+4t$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $Q(7)=30+4(7)=58$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The predicted value lies beyond the two observations, so it is an extrapolation; the units remain those of the measured quantity.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The linear model predicts approximately $58$ units at time 7.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The essential step is translating two observations into a rate and intercept before evaluating the model.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-005-S-01} — Standard}
\begin{problembox}
For a short planning horizon, a quantity associated with automobile aerodynamics, thermostat and ripple models is modeled by $Q(t)=65e^{0.045t}$, where $t$ is measured in years. Find the instantaneous growth rate at $t=5$ and interpret it.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which quantity is the independent variable?
\item What output is being modeled?
\item What units belong to the model parameters?
\item Over what domain is the model credible?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par The model is given; the requested quantity is the instantaneous rate of change.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $Q(t)$ is the modeled quantity and $t$ is time in years.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $Q(t)=65e^{0.045t}$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Differentiate because an instantaneous rate is requested.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $Q'(t)=65(0.045)e^{0.045t}=2.925e^{0.045t}$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $Q'(5)\approx 3.663$ units/year.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The derivative is positive, consistent with an increasing exponential model.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par At $t=5$, the quantity is increasing at approximately $3.663$ units per year.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The wording “instantaneous growth rate” identifies the derivative as the required operation.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-005-A-01} — Advanced}
\begin{problembox}
Measurements related to automobile aerodynamics, thermostat and ripple models are 40 units initially and 60.4 units after 6 time units. Assume $Q(t)=Q_0e^{rt}$. Estimate $r$, then determine when the model first reaches 72.0 units.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which quantity is the independent variable?
\item What output is being modeled?
\item What units belong to the model parameters?
\item Over what domain is the model credible?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Use two observations to identify an exponential model, then invert the model to find a threshold time.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $Q(t)$ is the measured quantity; $Q_0$ is its initial value; $r$ is the continuous growth rate.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $60.4=40e^{6r}$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par The modeling step uses logarithms to solve for an exponential parameter; the resulting model is then inverted.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $r=\frac1{6}\ln(60.4/40)\approx 0.06868$. For the threshold, $t=\ln(72.0/40)/r$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $t\approx 8.558$ time units.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par $Q(8.558)\approx 72.0$, so the model reproduces the requested threshold.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The fitted continuous rate is approximately $6.87\%$ per time unit, and the threshold is reached after about $8.56$ time units.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The key modeling decision is recognizing that two positive observations determine the exponential rate once the initial value is known.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\begin{reflectionbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which sentence or datum in these problems supplied the main mathematical constraint?
\item Which assumptions were essential to the model, and how would the model change if one were removed?

\part{Rates and Differential Calculus}
\chapter{Average velocity, speed and acceleration}\label{app:A006}
\textbf{Application ID:} \pid{A006}\quad \textbf{Source locus:} 1; 2.1–2.3; 12.3\quad \textbf{Source pages:} 44, 93, 116, 128, 132, 160, 180, 257, 910
\section*{Application Context}
This family treats change quantitatively. The derivative connects a mathematical model to an instantaneous physical, biological, or economic rate. The canonical application family is \emph{Average velocity, speed and acceleration}.
\section*{Calculus Toolkit}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}lX@{}}\toprule
Primary technique & Difference quotients, derivatives and higher derivatives \\
Prerequisites & functions, limits, derivative rules \\
Numerical extension & §5.7 (numerical derivatives) when derivative data are sampled \\
Analytical/numerical class & NUMERICAL-OPTIONAL \\
\bottomrule\end{tabularx}
\section*{Modeling Questions to Ask}\begin{itemize}
\item What quantity is changing?
\item With respect to which independent variable?
\item Is the requested rate average or instantaneous?
\item What units must the derivative have?
\end{itemize}
\begin{errorbox}Distinguish an average rate over an interval from an instantaneous derivative. Carry units through differentiation.\end{errorbox}
\section*{Problems}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-006-F-01} — Foundation}
\begin{problembox}
A quantity in a average velocity, speed and acceleration model is $Q(t)=5t^2+9t+10$. Find the instantaneous rate of change at $t=3$ and state its units as ‘quantity units per time unit.’
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What quantity is changing?
\item With respect to which independent variable?
\item Is the requested rate average or instantaneous?
\item What units must the derivative have?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par An instantaneous rate is the derivative evaluated at the specified time.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $t$ denotes time and $Q(t)$ the modeled quantity.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $Q(t)=5t^2+9t+10$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Differentiate $Q$ with respect to $t$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $Q'(t)=10t+9$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $Q'(3)=10(3)+9=39$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The derivative has units of quantity units per time unit.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The instantaneous rate is $39$ quantity units per time unit.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The phrase “instantaneous rate” is the decisive clue that a derivative is required.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-006-S-01} — Standard}
\begin{problembox}
For an application involving average velocity, speed and acceleration, let the total cost-like quantity be $C(q)=560+10q+0.030q^2$. At $q=46$, compute the marginal value $C'(q)$ and compare it with the average value $C(q)/q$.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What quantity is changing?
\item With respect to which independent variable?
\item Is the requested rate average or instantaneous?
\item What units must the derivative have?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par The problem contrasts a derivative-based marginal quantity with an average quantity.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $q$ is the operating level and $C(q)$ is the total quantity.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $C(q)=560+10q+0.030q^2$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Differentiate for the marginal value; divide by $q$ for the average.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $C'(q)=10+0.060q$, while $\bar C(q)=C(q)/q$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $C'(46)\approx 12.760$ and $\bar C(46)\approx 23.554$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par Both quantities have per-unit interpretations, but they answer different questions.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The marginal value is about $12.760$ per additional unit, whereas the average is about $23.554$ per unit.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par “Marginal” means derivative; “average” means total divided by production level.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-006-A-01} — Advanced}
\begin{problembox}
A sensitivity model for average velocity, speed and acceleration contains the response $R(x)=\sqrt{x}$. Around $x=106$, the input may increase by 1.4\%. Use differentials to estimate the change in $R$, and compare with the exact change.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What quantity is changing?
\item With respect to which independent variable?
\item Is the requested rate average or instantaneous?
\item What units must the derivative have?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Estimate a small output change produced by a small input perturbation.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $x=106$, $\Delta x=1.484$, and $R(x)=\sqrt x$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $dR=R'(x)\,dx=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{x}}dx$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use linearization/differentials because the input change is small relative to the base value.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $dR=\frac{1.484}{2\sqrt{106}}\approx 0.07207$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par The exact change is $\sqrt{107.484}-\sqrt{106}\approx 0.07182$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The absolute discrepancy is about $0.00025$, small relative to the change.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The differential estimate is $0.07207$; the exact change is $0.07182$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The derivative acts as a local sensitivity coefficient that converts a small input change into an output change.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\begin{reflectionbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which sentence or datum in these problems supplied the main mathematical constraint?
\item Which assumptions were essential to the model, and how would the model change if one were removed?
\item When would an analytical solution become inconvenient enough to justify a numerical method?
\end{enumerate}\end{reflectionbox}

\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Differentiate $Q$ with respect to $t$.\par\medskip

\chapter{Marginal cost, revenue, productivity and utility}\label{app:A011}
\textbf{Application ID:} \pid{A011}\quad \textbf{Source locus:} 13.3; 13.8–13.10\quad \textbf{Source pages:} 902–903, 949, 953, 959, 964
\section*{Application Context}
This family interprets derivatives as marginal quantities—local changes in cost, revenue, productivity, utility, or related response measures. The canonical application family is \emph{Marginal cost, revenue, productivity and utility}.
\section*{Calculus Toolkit}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}lX@{}}\toprule
Primary technique & Partial derivatives and marginal analysis \\
Prerequisites & derivatives, function interpretation, units \\
Numerical extension & Ch. 10 (optimization) and Ch. 15 (model fitting) for computational extensions \\
Analytical/numerical class & NUMERICAL-NATURAL \\
\bottomrule\end{tabularx}
\section*{Modeling Questions to Ask}\begin{itemize}
\item What economic/production quantity is differentiated?
\item At what operating level is the marginal value required?
\item Is the requested quantity marginal or average?
\item What units should the derivative carry?
\end{itemize}
\begin{errorbox}A marginal quantity is a derivative evaluated at a production level; it is not the same as an average quantity.\end{errorbox}
\section*{Problems}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-011-F-01} — Foundation}
\begin{problembox}
A quantity in a marginal cost, revenue, productivity and utility model is $Q(t)=10t^2+9t+10$. Find the instantaneous rate of change at $t=3$ and state its units as ‘quantity units per time unit.’
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What economic/production quantity is differentiated?
\item At what operating level is the marginal value required?
\item Is the requested quantity marginal or average?
\item What units should the derivative carry?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par An instantaneous rate is the derivative evaluated at the specified time.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $t$ denotes time and $Q(t)$ the modeled quantity.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $Q(t)=10t^2+9t+10$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Differentiate $Q$ with respect to $t$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $Q'(t)=20t+9$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $Q'(3)=20(3)+9=69$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The derivative has units of quantity units per time unit.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The instantaneous rate is $69$ quantity units per time unit.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The phrase “instantaneous rate” is the decisive clue that a derivative is required.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-011-S-01} — Standard}
\begin{problembox}
For an application involving marginal cost, revenue, productivity and utility, let the total cost-like quantity be $C(q)=610+10q+0.040q^2$. At $q=51$, compute the marginal value $C'(q)$ and compare it with the average value $C(q)/q$.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What economic/production quantity is differentiated?
\item At what operating level is the marginal value required?
\item Is the requested quantity marginal or average?
\item What units should the derivative carry?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par The problem contrasts a derivative-based marginal quantity with an average quantity.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $q$ is the operating level and $C(q)$ is the total quantity.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $C(q)=610+10q+0.040q^2$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Differentiate for the marginal value; divide by $q$ for the average.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $C'(q)=10+0.080q$, while $\bar C(q)=C(q)/q$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $C'(51)\approx 14.080$ and $\bar C(51)\approx 24.001$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par Both quantities have per-unit interpretations, but they answer different questions.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The marginal value is about $14.080$ per additional unit, whereas the average is about $24.001$ per unit.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par “Marginal” means derivative; “average” means total divided by production level.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-011-A-01} — Advanced}
\begin{problembox}
A sensitivity model for marginal cost, revenue, productivity and utility contains the response $R(x)=\sqrt{x}$. Around $x=111$, the input may increase by 1.5\%. Use differentials to estimate the change in $R$, and compare with the exact change.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What economic/production quantity is differentiated?
\item At what operating level is the marginal value required?
\item Is the requested quantity marginal or average?
\item What units should the derivative carry?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Estimate a small output change produced by a small input perturbation.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $x=111$, $\Delta x=1.665$, and $R(x)=\sqrt x$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $dR=R'(x)\,dx=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{x}}dx$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use linearization/differentials because the input change is small relative to the base value.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $dR=\frac{1.665}{2\sqrt{111}}\approx 0.07902$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par The exact change is $\sqrt{112.665}-\sqrt{111}\approx 0.07872$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The absolute discrepancy is about $0.00029$, small relative to the change.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The differential estimate is $0.07902$; the exact change is $0.07872$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The derivative acts as a local sensitivity coefficient that converts a small input change into an output change.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\begin{reflectionbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which sentence or datum in these problems supplied the main mathematical constraint?
\item Which assumptions were essential to the model, and how would the model change if one were removed?
\item When would an analytical solution become inconvenient enough to justify a numerical method?
\end{enumerate}\end{reflectionbox}
\chapter{Sales growth, declining sales, depreciation and investment growth}\label{app:A012}
\textbf{Application ID:} \pid{A012}\quad \textbf{Source locus:} 2; 5; 6; 9\quad \textbf{Source pages:} 143, 198, 243, 307, 349, 359, 418, 436, 606, 680
\section*{Application Context}
This family treats change quantitatively. The derivative connects a mathematical model to an instantaneous physical, biological, or economic rate. The canonical application family is \emph{Sales growth, declining sales, depreciation and investment growth}.
\section*{Calculus Toolkit}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}lX@{}}\toprule
Primary technique & Rates of change and exponential models \\
Prerequisites & functions, limits, derivative rules \\
Numerical extension & §5.7 (numerical derivatives) when derivative data are sampled \\
Analytical/numerical class & NUMERICAL-NATURAL \\
\bottomrule\end{tabularx}
\section*{Modeling Questions to Ask}\begin{itemize}
\item What quantity is changing?
\item With respect to which independent variable?
\item Is the requested rate average or instantaneous?
\item What units must the derivative have?
\end{itemize}
\begin{errorbox}Distinguish an average rate over an interval from an instantaneous derivative. Carry units through differentiation.\end{errorbox}
\section*{Problems}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-012-F-01} — Foundation}
\begin{problembox}
A quantity in a sales growth, declining sales, depreciation and investment growth model is $Q(t)=5t^2+10t+10$. Find the instantaneous rate of change at $t=3$ and state its units as ‘quantity units per time unit.’
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What quantity is changing?
\item With respect to which independent variable?
\item Is the requested rate average or instantaneous?
\item What units must the derivative have?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par An instantaneous rate is the derivative evaluated at the specified time.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $t$ denotes time and $Q(t)$ the modeled quantity.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $Q(t)=5t^2+10t+10$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Differentiate $Q$ with respect to $t$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $Q'(t)=10t+10$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $Q'(3)=10(3)+10=40$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The derivative has units of quantity units per time unit.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The instantaneous rate is $40$ quantity units per time unit.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The phrase “instantaneous rate” is the decisive clue that a derivative is required.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-012-S-01} — Standard}
\begin{problembox}
For an application involving sales growth, declining sales, depreciation and investment growth, let the total cost-like quantity be $C(q)=620+11q+0.030q^2$. At $q=52$, compute the marginal value $C'(q)$ and compare it with the average value $C(q)/q$.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What quantity is changing?
\item With respect to which independent variable?
\item Is the requested rate average or instantaneous?
\item What units must the derivative have?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par The problem contrasts a derivative-based marginal quantity with an average quantity.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $q$ is the operating level and $C(q)$ is the total quantity.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $C(q)=620+11q+0.030q^2$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Differentiate for the marginal value; divide by $q$ for the average.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $C'(q)=11+0.060q$, while $\bar C(q)=C(q)/q$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $C'(52)\approx 14.120$ and $\bar C(52)\approx 24.483$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par Both quantities have per-unit interpretations, but they answer different questions.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The marginal value is about $14.120$ per additional unit, whereas the average is about $24.483$ per unit.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par “Marginal” means derivative; “average” means total divided by production level.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-012-A-01} — Advanced}
\begin{problembox}
A sensitivity model for sales growth, declining sales, depreciation and investment growth contains the response $R(x)=\sqrt{x}$. Around $x=112$, the input may increase by 1.2\%. Use differentials to estimate the change in $R$, and compare with the exact change.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}

\item With respect to which independent variable?

\item What units must the derivative have?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Estimate a small output change produced by a small input perturbation.\par\medskip

\textbf{Construct the model}\par $dR=R'(x)\,dx=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{x}}dx$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use linearization/differentials because the input change is small relative to the base value.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $dR=\frac{1.344}{2\sqrt{112}}\approx 0.06350$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par The exact change is $\sqrt{113.344}-\sqrt{112}\approx 0.06331$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The absolute discrepancy is about $0.00019$, small relative to the change.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The differential estimate is $0.06350$; the exact change is $0.06331$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The derivative acts as a local sensitivity coefficient that converts a small input change into an output change.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\begin{reflectionbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which sentence or datum in these problems supplied the main mathematical constraint?
\item Which assumptions were essential to the model, and how would the model change if one were removed?
\item When would an analytical solution become inconvenient enough to justify a numerical method?
\end{enumerate}\end{reflectionbox}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-014-F-01} — Foundation}
\begin{problembox}
A quantity in a error and differential approximation model is $Q(t)=7t^2+12t+10$. Find the instantaneous rate of change at $t=3$ and state its units as ‘quantity units per time unit.’
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What quantity is changing?
\item With respect to which independent variable?
\item Is the requested rate average or instantaneous?
\item What units must the derivative have?

\textbf{Validate the result}\par The derivative has units of quantity units per time unit.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The instantaneous rate is $54$ quantity units per time unit.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The phrase “instantaneous rate” is the decisive clue that a derivative is required.\par\medskip

\part{Related Rates}
\chapter{Angular speed and angular rate of change}\label{app:A008}
\textbf{Application ID:} \pid{A008}\quad \textbf{Source locus:} P.4; 2.6; 5.8\quad \textbf{Source pages:} 38, 160, 162, 381
\section*{Application Context}
This family links two or more changing quantities through a constraint. Implicit differentiation converts the constraint into a relationship among rates. The canonical application family is \emph{Angular speed and angular rate of change}.
\section*{Calculus Toolkit}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}lX@{}}\toprule
Primary technique & Chain rule and related rates \\
Prerequisites & implicit differentiation, geometry, derivative rules \\
Numerical extension & §5.7 for numerical derivatives; Ch. 9 if an implicit state equation must be solved \\
Analytical/numerical class & NUMERICAL-OPTIONAL \\
\bottomrule\end{tabularx}
\section*{Modeling Questions to Ask}\begin{itemize}
\item Which quantities vary with time?
\item What equation connects them?
\item Which rate is known and which rate is requested?
\item At what instant should numerical values be substituted?
\end{itemize}
\begin{errorbox}Treat all changing quantities as functions of time, differentiate the relationship first, and only then substitute the instant-specific data.\end{errorbox}
\section*{Problems}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-008-F-01} — Foundation}
\begin{problembox}
In a angular speed and angular rate of change scenario, a circular quantity has radius $r=4$ m increasing at $dr/dt=0.40$ m/s. How fast is its area changing at that instant?
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which quantities vary with time?
\item What equation connects them?
\item Which rate is known and which rate is requested?
\item At what instant should numerical values be substituted?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Both radius and area change with time; the area rate is requested.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $r(t)$ is radius and $A(t)=\pi r(t)^2$ is area.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $A=\pi r^2$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Differentiate the geometric constraint with respect to time.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $\frac{dA}{dt}=2\pi r\frac{dr}{dt}$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $dA/dt=2\pi(4)(0.40)\approx 10.053\,\mathrm{m^2/s}$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The rate is positive because the radius is increasing.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The area is increasing at approximately $10.053\,\mathrm{m^2/s}$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par Differentiate before inserting the instantaneous radius and rate.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-008-S-01} — Standard}
\begin{problembox}
A rigid segment of length 10 m is used in a angular speed and angular rate of change model. One endpoint moves horizontally away from a reference point at 1.00 m/s while the other endpoint moves vertically. When the horizontal distance is 6 m, how fast is the vertical distance changing?
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which quantities vary with time?
\item What equation connects them?
\item Which rate is known and which rate is requested?
\item At what instant should numerical values be substituted?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par A fixed-length geometric constraint links two time-varying distances.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $x(t)$ is horizontal distance and $y(t)$ vertical distance.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $x^2+y^2=10^2$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use implicit differentiation with respect to time.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $2x\dot x+2y\dot y=0$, so $\dot y=-x\dot x/y$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $y=\sqrt{10^2-6^2}=8.000$ and $\dot y\approx -0.750$ m/s.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The negative sign means the vertical distance decreases while the horizontal distance grows.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The vertical distance is decreasing at approximately $0.750$ m/s.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The fixed length supplies the constraint; differentiating that constraint links the two rates.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-008-A-01} — Advanced}
\begin{problembox}
A nearly spherical volume in a angular speed and angular rate of change system is 1400 cm$^3$ and increasing at 21 cm$^3$/s. Assuming $V=\frac43\pi r^3$, estimate $dr/dt$ at that instant.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which quantities vary with time?
\item What equation connects them?
\item Which rate is known and which rate is requested?
\item At what instant should numerical values be substituted?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par The volume rate is known and the radial rate is requested.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $V(t)$ is volume and $r(t)$ radius.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $V=\frac43\pi r^3$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Differentiate the volume relation with respect to time.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $\dot V=4\pi r^2\dot r$, hence $\dot r=\dot V/(4\pi r^2)$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $r=(3(1400)/(4\pi))^{1/3}\approx 6.940$ cm, so $\dot r\approx 0.0347$ cm/s.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The radial rate is much smaller than the volume rate because volume scales cubically with radius.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The radius is increasing at approximately $0.0347$ cm/s.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par A related-rate problem often requires first computing an unreported geometric state variable at the specified instant.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\begin{reflectionbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which sentence or datum in these problems supplied the main mathematical constraint?
\item Which assumptions were essential to the model, and how would the model change if one were removed?
\item When would an analytical solution become inconvenient enough to justify a numerical method?
\end{enumerate}\end{reflectionbox}
\chapter{Moving ladder, moving shadow, air-traffic and angle-of-elevation problems}\label{app:A009}
\textbf{Application ID:} \pid{A009}\quad \textbf{Source locus:} 2.6; 10; 12\quad \textbf{Source pages:} 93, 155–164, 749, 854
\section*{Application Context}
This family links two or more changing quantities through a constraint. Implicit differentiation converts the constraint into a relationship among rates. The canonical application family is \emph{Moving ladder, moving shadow, air-traffic and angle-of-elevation problems}.
\section*{Calculus Toolkit}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}lX@{}}\toprule
Primary technique & Implicit differentiation and related rates \\
Prerequisites & implicit differentiation, geometry, derivative rules \\
Numerical extension & §5.7 for numerical derivatives; Ch. 9 if an implicit state equation must be solved \\
Analytical/numerical class & ANALYTICAL \\
\bottomrule\end{tabularx}
\section*{Modeling Questions to Ask}\begin{itemize}
\item Which quantities vary with time?
\item What equation connects them?
\item Which rate is known and which rate is requested?
\item At what instant should numerical values be substituted?
\end{itemize}
\begin{errorbox}Treat all changing quantities as functions of time, differentiate the relationship first, and only then substitute the instant-specific data.\end{errorbox}
\section*{Problems}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-009-F-01} — Foundation}
\begin{problembox}
In a moving ladder, moving shadow, air-traffic and angle-of-elevation problems scenario, a circular quantity has radius $r=5$ m increasing at $dr/dt=0.30$ m/s. How fast is its area changing at that instant?
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which quantities vary with time?
\item What equation connects them?
\item Which rate is known and which rate is requested?
\item At what instant should numerical values be substituted?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Both radius and area change with time; the area rate is requested.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $r(t)$ is radius and $A(t)=\pi r(t)^2$ is area.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $A=\pi r^2$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Differentiate the geometric constraint with respect to time.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $\frac{dA}{dt}=2\pi r\frac{dr}{dt}$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $dA/dt=2\pi(5)(0.30)\approx 9.425\,\mathrm{m^2/s}$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The rate is positive because the radius is increasing.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The area is increasing at approximately $9.425\,\mathrm{m^2/s}$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par Differentiate before inserting the instantaneous radius and rate.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-009-S-01} — Standard}
\begin{problembox}
A rigid segment of length 11 m is used in a moving ladder, moving shadow, air-traffic and angle-of-elevation problems model. One endpoint moves horizontally away from a reference point at 0.80 m/s while the other endpoint moves vertically. When the horizontal distance is 6 m, how fast is the vertical distance changing?
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which quantities vary with time?
\item What equation connects them?
\item Which rate is known and which rate is requested?
\item At what instant should numerical values be substituted?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par A fixed-length geometric constraint links two time-varying distances.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $x(t)$ is horizontal distance and $y(t)$ vertical distance.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $x^2+y^2=11^2$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use implicit differentiation with respect to time.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $2x\dot x+2y\dot y=0$, so $\dot y=-x\dot x/y$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $y=\sqrt{11^2-6^2}=9.220$ and $\dot y\approx -0.521$ m/s.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The negative sign means the vertical distance decreases while the horizontal distance grows.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The vertical distance is decreasing at approximately $0.521$ m/s.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The fixed length supplies the constraint; differentiating that constraint links the two rates.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-009-A-01} — Advanced}
\begin{problembox}
A nearly spherical volume in a moving ladder, moving shadow, air-traffic and angle-of-elevation problems system is 1450 cm$^3$ and increasing at 23 cm$^3$/s. Assuming $V=\frac43\pi r^3$, estimate $dr/dt$ at that instant.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which quantities vary with time?
\item What equation connects them?
\item Which rate is known and which rate is requested?
\item At what instant should numerical values be substituted?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par The volume rate is known and the radial rate is requested.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $V(t)$ is volume and $r(t)$ radius.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $V=\frac43\pi r^3$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Differentiate the volume relation with respect to time.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $\dot V=4\pi r^2\dot r$, hence $\dot r=\dot V/(4\pi r^2)$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $r=(3(1450)/(4\pi))^{1/3}\approx 7.021$ cm, so $\dot r\approx 0.0371$ cm/s.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The radial rate is much smaller than the volume rate because volume scales cubically with radius.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The radius is increasing at approximately $0.0371$ cm/s.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par A related-rate problem often requires first computing an unreported geometric state variable at the specified instant.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\begin{reflectionbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which sentence or datum in these problems supplied the main mathematical constraint?
\item Which assumptions were essential to the model, and how would the model change if one were removed?
\item When would an analytical solution become inconvenient enough to justify a numerical method?
\end{enumerate}\end{reflectionbox}

\part{Nonlinear Equations and Root Finding}
\chapter{Break-even point and cost–revenue intersections}\label{app:A015}
\textbf{Application ID:} \pid{A015}\quad \textbf{Source locus:} P.2; 7.1\quad \textbf{Source pages:} 9, 41, 452
\section*{Application Context}
This family reduces an application condition to a scalar or low-dimensional nonlinear equation. Feasible roots must satisfy both the equation and the application domain. The canonical application family is \emph{Break-even point and cost–revenue intersections}.
\section*{Calculus Toolkit}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}lX@{}}\toprule
Primary technique & Zeros/intersections of economic functions \\
Prerequisites & continuity, algebra, derivatives for Newton iteration \\
Numerical extension & Ch. 9, especially §§9.1–9.4 (bracketing, Brent, Newton) \\
Analytical/numerical class & NUMERICAL-NATURAL \\
\bottomrule\end{tabularx}
\section*{Modeling Questions to Ask}\begin{itemize}
\item What equation must equal zero?
\item What interval is physically feasible?
\item Can a root be bracketed?
\item How will the numerical answer be checked?
\end{itemize}
\begin{errorbox}A numerical root is meaningful only if the equation models the application and the root lies in the feasible domain. Check the residual.\end{errorbox}
\section*{Problems}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-015-F-01} — Foundation}
\begin{problembox}
A simplified equation arising in break-even point and cost–revenue intersections is $x^2+x-13=0$ with $x>0$. Show that the positive root is bracketed between two consecutive integers and compute it exactly.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What equation must equal zero?
\item What interval is physically feasible?
\item Can a root be bracketed?
\item How will the numerical answer be checked?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par The feasible solution is the positive zero of a scalar nonlinear equation.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $f(x)=x^2+x-13$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par Solve $f(x)=0$ subject to $x>0$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Bracket the root by a sign change, then use the quadratic formula because this particular model is algebraic.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $f(3)=-1$ and $f(4)=7$, so a root lies in $(3,4)$. The positive root is $(-1+\sqrt{53})/2$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $x\approx 3.14005$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par $f(3.14005)\approx 0$ and $x>0$.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The feasible root is $x\approx 3.14005$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par Bracketing confirms a feasible root before a numerical solver is used.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-015-S-01} — Standard}
\begin{problembox}
A calibration equation for break-even point and cost–revenue intersections is $e^{-x}=x/2.0$. Use bisection on $[0,2]$ until the interval width is below $10^{-4}$, and report the solution.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What equation must equal zero?
\item What interval is physically feasible?
\item Can a root be bracketed?
\item How will the numerical answer be checked?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par A transcendental equation must be solved numerically.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $f(x)=e^{-x}-x/2.0$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par The desired state satisfies $f(x)=0$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use bisection because $f$ is continuous and the endpoint signs differ.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $f(0)=1>0$ and $f(2)=-0.8647<0$. Repeated midpoint selection preserves the sign-changing bracket.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par Bisection gives $x\approx 0.85261$ with a final bracket narrower than $10^{-4}$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par $|f(0.85261)|\approx 6.66e-16$.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The calibrated value is approximately $x=0.85261$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The continuity and sign change justify bisection independently of derivative information.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-015-A-01} — Advanced}
\begin{problembox}
For a nonlinear model associated with break-even point and cost–revenue intersections, solve $\cos x=x/3$ using Newton's method from $x_0=1$. Carry out four iterations and check the residual.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What equation must equal zero?
\item What interval is physically feasible?
\item Can a root be bracketed?
\item How will the numerical answer be checked?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par The equation is nonlinear and a derivative-based iterative root solver is requested.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $f(x)=\cos x-x/3$ and $f'(x)=-\sin x-1/3$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par Newton iteration is $x_{n+1}=x_n-f(x_n)/f'(x_n)$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Newton’s method uses the tangent-line linearization of $f$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par Starting with $x_0=1$, apply the recurrence repeatedly.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $x_1=1.176173$, $x_2=1.170127$, $x_3=1.170121$, $x_4=1.170121$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par $|f(x_4)|\approx 1.67e-16$.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The numerical root is $x\approx 1.170121$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par Unlike bisection, Newton’s method is fast near a good root but depends on derivative behavior and the starting value.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\begin{reflectionbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which sentence or datum in these problems supplied the main mathematical constraint?
\item Which assumptions were essential to the model, and how would the model change if one were removed?
\item When would an analytical solution become inconvenient enough to justify a numerical method?
\end{enumerate}\end{reflectionbox}

\begin{problembox}
A simplified equation arising in optical/refraction and geometric intersection problems is $x^2+x-15=0$ with $x>0$. Show that the positive root is bracketed between two consecutive integers and compute it exactly.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What equation must equal zero?
\item What interval is physically feasible?
\item Can a root be bracketed?
\item How will the numerical answer be checked?

\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par The feasible solution is the positive zero of a scalar nonlinear equation.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $f(x)=x^2+x-15$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par Solve $f(x)=0$ subject to $x>0$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Bracket the root by a sign change, then use the quadratic formula because this particular model is algebraic.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $f(3)=-3$ and $f(4)=5$, so a root lies in $(3,4)$. The positive root is $(-1+\sqrt{61})/2$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $x\approx 3.40512$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par $f(3.40512)\approx 0$ and $x>0$.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The feasible root is $x\approx 3.40512$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par Bracketing confirms a feasible root before a numerical solver is used.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-017-S-01} — Standard}
\begin{problembox}
A calibration equation for optical/refraction and geometric intersection problems is $e^{-x}=x/2.2$. Use bisection on $[0,2]$ until the interval width is below $10^{-4}$, and report the solution.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What equation must equal zero?
\item What interval is physically feasible?
\item Can a root be bracketed?
\item How will the numerical answer be checked?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par A transcendental equation must be solved numerically.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $f(x)=e^{-x}-x/2.2$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par The desired state satisfies $f(x)=0$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use bisection because $f$ is continuous and the endpoint signs differ.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $f(0)=1>0$ and $f(2)=-0.7738<0$. Repeated midpoint selection preserves the sign-changing bracket.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par Bisection gives $x\approx 0.89707$ with a final bracket narrower than $10^{-4}$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par $|f(0.89707)|\approx 4.44e-16$.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The calibrated value is approximately $x=0.89707$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The continuity and sign change justify bisection independently of derivative information.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-017-A-01} — Advanced}
\begin{problembox}
For a nonlinear model associated with optical/refraction and geometric intersection problems, solve $\cos x=x/5$ using Newton's method from $x_0=1$. Carry out four iterations and check the residual.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What equation must equal zero?
\item What interval is physically feasible?
\item Can a root be bracketed?
\item How will the numerical answer be checked?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par The equation is nonlinear and a derivative-based iterative root solver is requested.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $f(x)=\cos x-x/5$ and $f'(x)=-\sin x-1/5$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par Newton iteration is $x_{n+1}=x_n-f(x_n)/f'(x_n)$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Newton’s method uses the tangent-line linearization of $f$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par Starting with $x_0=1$, apply the recurrence repeatedly.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $x_1=1.326752$, $x_2=1.306484$, $x_3=1.306440$, $x_4=1.306440$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par $|f(x_4)|\approx 5.55e-17$.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The numerical root is $x\approx 1.306440$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par Unlike bisection, Newton’s method is fast near a good root but depends on derivative behavior and the starting value.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\begin{reflectionbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which sentence or datum in these problems supplied the main mathematical constraint?
\item Which assumptions were essential to the model, and how would the model change if one were removed?
\item When would an analytical solution become inconvenient enough to justify a numerical method?
\end{enumerate}\end{reflectionbox}
\chapter{Polynomial design/intersection and machine-part equations}\label{app:A018}

\section*{Application Context}

\bottomrule\end{tabularx}
\section*{Modeling Questions to Ask}\begin{itemize}
\item What equation must equal zero?
\item What interval is physically feasible?
\item Can a root be bracketed?
\item How will the numerical answer be checked?
\end{itemize}
\begin{errorbox}A numerical root is meaningful only if the equation models the application and the root lies in the feasible domain. Check the residual.\end{errorbox}

\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}

\item Can a root be bracketed?

\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Bracket the root by a sign change, then use the quadratic formula because this particular model is algebraic.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $f(2)=-4$ and $f(3)=2$, so a root lies in $(2,3)$. The positive root is $(-1+\sqrt{41})/2$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $x\approx 2.70156$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par $f(2.70156)\approx 0$ and $x>0$.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The feasible root is $x\approx 2.70156$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par Bracketing confirms a feasible root before a numerical solver is used.\par\medskip

\chapter{Maximum area, minimum area, perimeter, length and surface area}\label{app:A019}
\textbf{Application ID:} \pid{A019}\quad \textbf{Source locus:} 3.7; 13.8–13.10\quad \textbf{Source pages:} 43, 221–226, 244, 968
\section*{Application Context}
This family turns design or decision questions into objective functions subject to geometric, physical, or economic constraints. The canonical application family is \emph{Maximum area, minimum area, perimeter, length and surface area}.
\section*{Calculus Toolkit}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}lX@{}}\toprule
Primary technique & Scalar and constrained optimization \\
Prerequisites & derivatives, critical points, constraints \\
Numerical extension & Ch. 10, especially §§10.2–10.9 \\
Analytical/numerical class & NUMERICAL-NATURAL \\
\bottomrule\end{tabularx}
\section*{Modeling Questions to Ask}\begin{itemize}
\item What is the objective function?
\item What constraints define the feasible set?
\item Can the objective be reduced to one variable?
\item How will the extremum be verified?
\end{itemize}
\begin{errorbox}A critical point is only a candidate. Enforce constraints and compare endpoints or otherwise establish that the requested extremum has been found.\end{errorbox}
\section*{Problems}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-019-F-01} — Foundation}
\begin{problembox}
A design related to maximum area, minimum area, perimeter, length and surface area uses a rectangular region with total perimeter 68 m. Determine the dimensions that maximize area.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What is the objective function?
\item What constraints define the feasible set?
\item Can the objective be reduced to one variable?
\item How will the extremum be verified?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Maximize rectangle area subject to a fixed perimeter.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par Let $x$ and $y$ be side lengths.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $2x+2y=68$, so $y=34.0-x$ and $A(x)=x(34.0-x)$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Differentiate the one-variable objective and test the critical point.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $A'(x)=34.0-2x=0$ gives $x=17.0$ and therefore $y=17.0$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $x=y=17.00$ m and $A_{\max}=289.00$ m$^2$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par $A''(x)=-2<0$, so the critical point is a maximum.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The area is maximized by a square $17.00$ m on each side.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The constraint reduces the two-variable geometry to a one-variable objective.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-019-S-01} — Standard}
\begin{problembox}
A cost model for maximum area, minimum area, perimeter, length and surface area is $C(x)=2950/x+88+0.6x$ for $x>0$. Find the operating value $x$ that minimizes cost and the minimum cost.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What is the objective function?
\item What constraints define the feasible set?
\item Can the objective be reduced to one variable?
\item How will the extremum be verified?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Minimize a positive-domain cost function.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $x>0$ is the decision variable and $C(x)$ the cost.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $C(x)=2950x^{-1}+88+0.6x$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Set the derivative equal to zero and verify convexity.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $C'(x)=-2950/x^2+0.6=0$, so $x=\sqrt{2950/0.6}$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $x\approx 70.119$ and $C(x)\approx 172.143$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par $C''(x)=2(2950)/x^3>0$ for $x>0$, so this is the unique minimum.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The minimum occurs at $x\approx 70.119$, with minimum cost approximately $172.143$ units.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par Convexity turns a stationary point into a verified global minimum on the positive domain.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-019-A-01} — Advanced}
\begin{problembox}
A two-factor design in maximum area, minimum area, perimeter, length and surface area has nonnegative decision variables $x$ and $y$ constrained by $x+y=24$. Maximize the performance measure $P=xy$ using a Lagrange-multiplier argument.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What is the objective function?
\item What constraints define the feasible set?
\item Can the objective be reduced to one variable?
\item How will the extremum be verified?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Maximize a two-variable objective under one equality constraint.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $P(x,y)=xy$ and $g(x,y)=x+y-24=0$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $\nabla P=\lambda\nabla g$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use Lagrange multipliers because the feasible set is an equality-constrained curve.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $\nabla P=(y,x)$ and $\nabla g=(1,1)$, so $x=y=\lambda$. With $x+y=24$, $x=y=12$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $P_{\max}=144.00$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par On the closed line segment in the nonnegative quadrant, the endpoints give zero while the interior stationary point gives positive performance.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The maximum occurs at $x=y=12.00$, with $P=144.00$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The multiplier equations encode the fact that, at a constrained extremum, the objective gradient is normal to the feasible curve.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\begin{reflectionbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which sentence or datum in these problems supplied the main mathematical constraint?
\item Which assumptions were essential to the model, and how would the model change if one were removed?
\item When would an analytical solution become inconvenient enough to justify a numerical method?
\end{enumerate}\end{reflectionbox}
