\textbf{Construct the model}\par $C(x)=3050x^{-1}+82+0.5x$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Set the derivative equal to zero and verify convexity.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $C'(x)=-3050/x^2+0.5=0$, so $x=\sqrt{3050/0.5}$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $x\approx 78.102$ and $C(x)\approx 160.102$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par $C''(x)=2(3050)/x^3>0$ for $x>0$, so this is the unique minimum.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The minimum occurs at $x\approx 78.102$, with minimum cost approximately $160.102$ units.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par Convexity turns a stationary point into a verified global minimum on the positive domain.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-021-A-01} — Advanced}
\begin{problembox}
A two-factor design in minimum distance, time and travel-path optimization has nonnegative decision variables $x$ and $y$ constrained by $x+y=21$. Maximize the performance measure $P=xy$ using a Lagrange-multiplier argument.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What is the objective function?
\item What constraints define the feasible set?
\item Can the objective be reduced to one variable?
\item How will the extremum be verified?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Maximize a two-variable objective under one equality constraint.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $P(x,y)=xy$ and $g(x,y)=x+y-21=0$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $\nabla P=\lambda\nabla g$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use Lagrange multipliers because the feasible set is an equality-constrained curve.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $\nabla P=(y,x)$ and $\nabla g=(1,1)$, so $x=y=\lambda$. With $x+y=21$, $x=y=10.5$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $P_{\max}=110.25$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par On the closed line segment in the nonnegative quadrant, the endpoints give zero while the interior stationary point gives positive performance.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The maximum occurs at $x=y=10.50$, with $P=110.25$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The multiplier equations encode the fact that, at a constrained extremum, the objective gradient is normal to the feasible curve.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\begin{reflectionbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which sentence or datum in these problems supplied the main mathematical constraint?
\item Which assumptions were essential to the model, and how would the model change if one were removed?
\item When would an analytical solution become inconvenient enough to justify a numerical method?
\end{enumerate}\end{reflectionbox}
\chapter{Beam strength, illumination, engine efficiency and mechanical design}\label{app:A022}

\section*{Application Context}
This family turns design or decision questions into objective functions subject to geometric, physical, or economic constraints. The canonical application family is \emph{Beam strength, illumination, engine efficiency and mechanical design}.
\section*{Calculus Toolkit}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}lX@{}}\toprule
Primary technique & Engineering objective functions \\
Prerequisites & derivatives, critical points, constraints \\
Numerical extension & Ch. 10, especially §§10.2–10.9 \\
Analytical/numerical class & NUMERICAL-NATURAL \\

\section*{Modeling Questions to Ask}\begin{itemize}
\item What is the objective function?
\item What constraints define the feasible set?
\item Can the objective be reduced to one variable?
\item How will the extremum be verified?
\end{itemize}
\begin{errorbox}A critical point is only a candidate. Enforce constraints and compare endpoints or otherwise establish that the requested extremum has been found.\end{errorbox}
\section*{Problems}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-022-F-01} — Foundation}
\begin{problembox}
A design related to beam strength, illumination, engine efficiency and mechanical design uses a rectangular region with total perimeter 64 m. Determine the dimensions that maximize area.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What is the objective function?
\item What constraints define the feasible set?
\item Can the objective be reduced to one variable?
\item How will the extremum be verified?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Maximize rectangle area subject to a fixed perimeter.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par Let $x$ and $y$ be side lengths.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $2x+2y=64$, so $y=32.0-x$ and $A(x)=x(32.0-x)$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Differentiate the one-variable objective and test the critical point.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $A'(x)=32.0-2x=0$ gives $x=16.0$ and therefore $y=16.0$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $x=y=16.00$ m and $A_{\max}=256.00$ m$^2$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par $A''(x)=-2<0$, so the critical point is a maximum.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The area is maximized by a square $16.00$ m on each side.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The constraint reduces the two-variable geometry to a one-variable objective.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}

\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $\nabla P=(y,x)$ and $\nabla g=(1,1)$, so $x=y=\lambda$. With $x+y=20$, $x=y=10$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $P_{\max}=100.00$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par On the closed line segment in the nonnegative quadrant, the endpoints give zero while the interior stationary point gives positive performance.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The maximum occurs at $x=y=10.00$, with $P=100.00$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The multiplier equations encode the fact that, at a constrained extremum, the objective gradient is normal to the feasible curve.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\begin{reflectionbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which sentence or datum in these problems supplied the main mathematical constraint?
\item Which assumptions were essential to the model, and how would the model change if one were removed?
\item When would an analytical solution become inconvenient enough to justify a numerical method?
\end{enumerate}\end{reflectionbox}
\chapter{Building a pipeline}\label{app:A026}
\textbf{Application ID:} \pid{A026}\quad \textbf{Source locus:} 13.9\quad \textbf{Source pages:} 955
\section*{Application Context}
This family turns design or decision questions into objective functions subject to geometric, physical, or economic constraints. The canonical application family is \emph{Building a pipeline}.
\section*{Calculus Toolkit}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}lX@{}}\toprule
Primary technique & Multivariable geometric optimization \\
Prerequisites & derivatives, critical points, constraints \\
Numerical extension & Ch. 10, especially §§10.2–10.9 \\
Analytical/numerical class & NUMERICAL-NATURAL \\
\bottomrule\end{tabularx}
\section*{Modeling Questions to Ask}\begin{itemize}
\item What is the objective function?
\item What constraints define the feasible set?
\item Can the objective be reduced to one variable?
\item How will the extremum be verified?
\end{itemize}
\begin{errorbox}A critical point is only a candidate. Enforce constraints and compare endpoints or otherwise establish that the requested extremum has been found.\end{errorbox}
\section*{Problems}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-026-F-01} — Foundation}
\begin{problembox}
A design related to building a pipeline uses a rectangular region with total perimeter 62 m. Determine the dimensions that maximize area.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What is the objective function?
\item What constraints define the feasible set?
\item Can the objective be reduced to one variable?
\item How will the extremum be verified?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Maximize rectangle area subject to a fixed perimeter.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par Let $x$ and $y$ be side lengths.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $2x+2y=62$, so $y=31.0-x$ and $A(x)=x(31.0-x)$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Differentiate the one-variable objective and test the critical point.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $A'(x)=31.0-2x=0$ gives $x=15.5$ and therefore $y=15.5$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $x=y=15.50$ m and $A_{\max}=240.25$ m$^2$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par $A''(x)=-2<0$, so the critical point is a maximum.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The area is maximized by a square $15.50$ m on each side.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The constraint reduces the two-variable geometry to a one-variable objective.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-026-S-01} — Standard}
\begin{problembox}
A cost model for building a pipeline is $C(x)=3300/x+82+0.7x$ for $x>0$. Find the operating value $x$ that minimizes cost and the minimum cost.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What is the objective function?
\item What constraints define the feasible set?
\item Can the objective be reduced to one variable?
\item How will the extremum be verified?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Minimize a positive-domain cost function.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $x>0$ is the decision variable and $C(x)$ the cost.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $C(x)=3300x^{-1}+82+0.7x$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Set the derivative equal to zero and verify convexity.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $C'(x)=-3300/x^2+0.7=0$, so $x=\sqrt{3300/0.7}$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $x\approx 68.661$ and $C(x)\approx 178.125$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par $C''(x)=2(3300)/x^3>0$ for $x>0$, so this is the unique minimum.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The minimum occurs at $x\approx 68.661$, with minimum cost approximately $178.125$ units.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par Convexity turns a stationary point into a verified global minimum on the positive domain.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-026-A-01} — Advanced}
\begin{problembox}
A two-factor design in building a pipeline has nonnegative decision variables $x$ and $y$ constrained by $x+y=21$. Maximize the performance measure $P=xy$ using a Lagrange-multiplier argument.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What is the objective function?
\item What constraints define the feasible set?
\item Can the objective be reduced to one variable?
\item How will the extremum be verified?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Maximize a two-variable objective under one equality constraint.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $P(x,y)=xy$ and $g(x,y)=x+y-21=0$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $\nabla P=\lambda\nabla g$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use Lagrange multipliers because the feasible set is an equality-constrained curve.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $\nabla P=(y,x)$ and $\nabla g=(1,1)$, so $x=y=\lambda$. With $x+y=21$, $x=y=10.5$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $P_{\max}=110.25$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par On the closed line segment in the nonnegative quadrant, the endpoints give zero while the interior stationary point gives positive performance.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The maximum occurs at $x=y=10.50$, with $P=110.25$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The multiplier equations encode the fact that, at a constrained extremum, the objective gradient is normal to the feasible curve.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\begin{reflectionbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which sentence or datum in these problems supplied the main mathematical constraint?
\item Which assumptions were essential to the model, and how would the model change if one were removed?
\item When would an analytical solution become inconvenient enough to justify a numerical method?
\end{enumerate}\end{reflectionbox}
\part{Integral Applications}
\chapter{Area and area between curves}\label{app:A027}
\textbf{Application ID:} \pid{A027}\quad \textbf{Source locus:} 4; 7.1\quad \textbf{Source pages:} 120, 130, 157, 240, 260, 444–453, 607, 678
\section*{Application Context}
This family models totals by accumulating infinitesimal contributions. Correct bounds and units are as important as evaluating the antiderivative or quadrature. The canonical application family is \emph{Area and area between curves}.
\section*{Calculus Toolkit}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}lX@{}}\toprule
Primary technique & Definite integration and accumulation \\
Prerequisites & definite integrals, units, geometric/physical modeling \\
Numerical extension & Ch. 4, especially §§4.1–4.8 \\
Analytical/numerical class & NUMERICAL-NATURAL \\
\bottomrule\end{tabularx}
\section*{Modeling Questions to Ask}\begin{itemize}
\item What infinitesimal contribution is accumulated?
\item What variable should be integrated over?
\item What are the correct bounds?
\item What units should the final integral have?
\end{itemize}
\begin{errorbox}Build the infinitesimal contribution before integrating. Check bounds and units of the integrand and final accumulated quantity.\end{errorbox}
\section*{Problems}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-027-F-01} — Foundation}
\begin{problembox}
A cumulative quantity in area and area between curves is modeled by the rate $r(x)=x^2+2$ over $0\le x\le 9$. Find the total accumulated amount.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What infinitesimal contribution is accumulated?
\item What variable should be integrated over?
\item What are the correct bounds?
\item What units should the final integral have?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par A rate density is accumulated over an interval.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $r(x)$ is the contribution per unit $x$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $Q=\int_0^{9}(x^2+2)\,dx$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use a definite integral because the total is an accumulation of a continuously varying rate.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $Q=[x^3/3+2x]_0^{9}$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $Q=261.000$ accumulated units.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The integrand is positive, so the positive total is consistent; units equal rate-units times $x$-units.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The accumulated amount is $261.000$ units.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The integral is built from “rate × small interval” contributions.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-027-S-01} — Standard}
\begin{problembox}
In a area and area between curves application, liquid fills a vertical prismatic tank of height 8 m and horizontal cross-sectional area 2 m$^2$. Use a weight density of 9.8 kN/m$^3$. Find the work required to pump all liquid just to the top edge.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What infinitesimal contribution is accumulated?
\item What variable should be integrated over?
\item What are the correct bounds?
\item What units should the final integral have?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Each horizontal slice has a different lifting distance.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par Let $y$ be height above the tank bottom; a slice of thickness $dy$ has volume $A\,dy$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $dW=(9.8)(2)(8-y)\,dy$ kN·m.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Integrate slice work from bottom to top.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $W=9.8(2)\int_0^{8}(8-y)\,dy=9.8(2)(8^2/2)$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $W\approx 627.200$ kJ.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The bottom slices travel farthest and the top slice travels essentially zero distance; the integral reflects this.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par Approximately $627.200$ kJ of work is required.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The difficult step is modeling the differential work of a thin slice, not evaluating the integral.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-027-A-01} — Advanced}
\begin{problembox}
A non-elementary accumulation arising in area and area between curves is $I=\int_0^1 e^{-x^2}\,dx$. Approximate $I$ with composite Simpson's rule using four equal subintervals, and explain why a numerical method is appropriate.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What infinitesimal contribution is accumulated?
\item What variable should be integrated over?
\item What are the correct bounds?
\item What units should the final integral have?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par The definite integral has no elementary antiderivative, so numerical quadrature is natural.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $f(x)=e^{-x^2}$, $a=0$, $b=1$, $n=4$, and $h=0.25$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $I\approx\frac{h}{3}[f_0+4f_1+2f_2+4f_3+f_4]$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use composite Simpson quadrature; this is a numerical extension of definite integration.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par Evaluate the integrand at $x=0,0.25,0.5,0.75,1$ and form the weighted sum.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $I\approx 0.74685538$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par Since $e^{-1}\le e^{-x^2}\le1$ on $[0,1]$, the result must lie between $e^{-1}$ and 1; it does.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The four-subinterval Simpson approximation is $I\approx 0.74685538$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par Recognizing the absence of an elementary antiderivative tells us to switch from symbolic integration to quadrature.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\begin{reflectionbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which sentence or datum in these problems supplied the main mathematical constraint?
\item Which assumptions were essential to the model, and how would the model change if one were removed?
\item When would an analytical solution become inconvenient enough to justify a numerical method?
\end{enumerate}\end{reflectionbox}

\chapter{Surface area and surfaces of revolution}\label{app:A030}
\textbf{Application ID:} \pid{A030}\quad \textbf{Source locus:} 7.4; 14.5\quad \textbf{Source pages:} 157, 162, 241, 474–484, 578, 1006–1012
\section*{Application Context}
This family models totals by accumulating infinitesimal contributions. Correct bounds and units are as important as evaluating the antiderivative or quadrature. The canonical application family is \emph{Surface area and surfaces of revolution}.
\section*{Calculus Toolkit}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}lX@{}}\toprule
Primary technique & Surface-area integrals \\
Prerequisites & definite integrals, units, geometric/physical modeling \\
Numerical extension & Ch. 4, especially §§4.1–4.8 \\
Analytical/numerical class & NUMERICAL-NATURAL \\
\bottomrule\end{tabularx}
\section*{Modeling Questions to Ask}\begin{itemize}
\item What infinitesimal contribution is accumulated?
\item What variable should be integrated over?
\item What are the correct bounds?
\item What units should the final integral have?
\end{itemize}
\begin{errorbox}Build the infinitesimal contribution before integrating. Check bounds and units of the integrand and final accumulated quantity.\end{errorbox}
\section*{Problems}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-030-F-01} — Foundation}
\begin{problembox}
A cumulative quantity in surface area and surfaces of revolution is modeled by the rate $r(x)=x^2+2$ over $0\le x\le 8$. Find the total accumulated amount.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What infinitesimal contribution is accumulated?
\item What variable should be integrated over?
\item What are the correct bounds?
\item What units should the final integral have?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par A rate density is accumulated over an interval.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $r(x)$ is the contribution per unit $x$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $Q=\int_0^{8}(x^2+2)\,dx$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use a definite integral because the total is an accumulation of a continuously varying rate.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $Q=[x^3/3+2x]_0^{8}$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $Q=186.667$ accumulated units.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The integrand is positive, so the positive total is consistent; units equal rate-units times $x$-units.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The accumulated amount is $186.667$ units.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The integral is built from “rate × small interval” contributions.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-030-S-01} — Standard}
\begin{problembox}
In a surface area and surfaces of revolution application, liquid fills a vertical prismatic tank of height 7 m and horizontal cross-sectional area 2 m$^2$. Use a weight density of 9.8 kN/m$^3$. Find the work required to pump all liquid just to the top edge.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What infinitesimal contribution is accumulated?
\item What variable should be integrated over?
\item What are the correct bounds?
\item What units should the final integral have?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Each horizontal slice has a different lifting distance.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par Let $y$ be height above the tank bottom; a slice of thickness $dy$ has volume $A\,dy$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $dW=(9.8)(2)(7-y)\,dy$ kN·m.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Integrate slice work from bottom to top.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $W=9.8(2)\int_0^{7}(7-y)\,dy=9.8(2)(7^2/2)$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $W\approx 480.200$ kJ.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The bottom slices travel farthest and the top slice travels essentially zero distance; the integral reflects this.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par Approximately $480.200$ kJ of work is required.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The difficult step is modeling the differential work of a thin slice, not evaluating the integral.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-030-A-01} — Advanced}
\begin{problembox}
A non-elementary accumulation arising in surface area and surfaces of revolution is $I=\int_0^1 e^{-x^2}\,dx$. Approximate $I$ with composite Simpson's rule using four equal subintervals, and explain why a numerical method is appropriate.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What infinitesimal contribution is accumulated?
\item What variable should be integrated over?
\item What are the correct bounds?
\item What units should the final integral have?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par The definite integral has no elementary antiderivative, so numerical quadrature is natural.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $f(x)=e^{-x^2}$, $a=0$, $b=1$, $n=4$, and $h=0.25$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $I\approx\frac{h}{3}[f_0+4f_1+2f_2+4f_3+f_4]$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use composite Simpson quadrature; this is a numerical extension of definite integration.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par Evaluate the integrand at $x=0,0.25,0.5,0.75,1$ and form the weighted sum.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $I\approx 0.74685538$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par Since $e^{-1}\le e^{-x^2}\le1$ on $[0,1]$, the result must lie between $e^{-1}$ and 1; it does.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The four-subinterval Simpson approximation is $I\approx 0.74685538$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par Recognizing the absence of an elementary antiderivative tells us to switch from symbolic integration to quadrature.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\begin{reflectionbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which sentence or datum in these problems supplied the main mathematical constraint?
\item Which assumptions were essential to the model, and how would the model change if one were removed?
\item When would an analytical solution become inconvenient enough to justify a numerical method?
\end{enumerate}\end{reflectionbox}

\textbf{Solution insight}\par The integral is built from “rate × small interval” contributions.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-034-S-01} — Standard}
\begin{problembox}
In a consumer and producer surplus application, liquid fills a vertical prismatic tank of height 7 m and horizontal cross-sectional area 3 m$^2$. Use a weight density of 9.8 kN/m$^3$. Find the work required to pump all liquid just to the top edge.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What infinitesimal contribution is accumulated?
\item What variable should be integrated over?
\item What are the correct bounds?
\item What units should the final integral have?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Each horizontal slice has a different lifting distance.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par Let $y$ be height above the tank bottom; a slice of thickness $dy$ has volume $A\,dy$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $dW=(9.8)(3)(7-y)\,dy$ kN·m.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Integrate slice work from bottom to top.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $W=9.8(3)\int_0^{7}(7-y)\,dy=9.8(3)(7^2/2)$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $W\approx 720.300$ kJ.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The bottom slices travel farthest and the top slice travels essentially zero distance; the integral reflects this.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par Approximately $720.300$ kJ of work is required.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The difficult step is modeling the differential work of a thin slice, not evaluating the integral.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-034-A-01} — Advanced}
\begin{problembox}
A non-elementary accumulation arising in consumer and producer surplus is $I=\int_0^1 e^{-x^2}\,dx$. Approximate $I$ with composite Simpson's rule using four equal subintervals, and explain why a numerical method is appropriate.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What infinitesimal contribution is accumulated?
\item What variable should be integrated over?
\item What are the correct bounds?
\item What units should the final integral have?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par The definite integral has no elementary antiderivative, so numerical quadrature is natural.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $f(x)=e^{-x^2}$, $a=0$, $b=1$, $n=4$, and $h=0.25$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $I\approx\frac{h}{3}[f_0+4f_1+2f_2+4f_3+f_4]$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use composite Simpson quadrature; this is a numerical extension of definite integration.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par Evaluate the integrand at $x=0,0.25,0.5,0.75,1$ and form the weighted sum.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $I\approx 0.74685538$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par Since $e^{-1}\le e^{-x^2}\le1$ on $[0,1]$, the result must lie between $e^{-1}$ and 1; it does.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The four-subinterval Simpson approximation is $I\approx 0.74685538$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par Recognizing the absence of an elementary antiderivative tells us to switch from symbolic integration to quadrature.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\begin{reflectionbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which sentence or datum in these problems supplied the main mathematical constraint?
\item Which assumptions were essential to the model, and how would the model change if one were removed?
\item When would an analytical solution become inconvenient enough to justify a numerical method?
\end{enumerate}\end{reflectionbox}

\begin{problembox}
A cumulative quantity in average temperature, production and profit is modeled by the rate $r(x)=x^2+4$ over $0\le x\le 9$. Find the total accumulated amount.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What infinitesimal contribution is accumulated?
\item What variable should be integrated over?
\item What are the correct bounds?
\item What units should the final integral have?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par A rate density is accumulated over an interval.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $r(x)$ is the contribution per unit $x$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $Q=\int_0^{9}(x^2+4)\,dx$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use a definite integral because the total is an accumulation of a continuously varying rate.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $Q=[x^3/3+4x]_0^{9}$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $Q=279.000$ accumulated units.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The integrand is positive, so the positive total is consistent; units equal rate-units times $x$-units.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The accumulated amount is $279.000$ units.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The integral is built from “rate × small interval” contributions.\par\medskip

\part{Approximation, Series, and Special Models}
\chapter{Topographic maps, terrain and scattered spatial data}\label{app:A037}
\textbf{Application ID:} \pid{A037}\quad \textbf{Source locus:} 2; 13\quad \textbf{Source pages:} 150, 875, 929, 930
\section*{Application Context}
This family uses interpolation, series, transformations, and approximation to evaluate functions or represent data when direct formulas are inconvenient. The canonical application family is \emph{Topographic maps, terrain and scattered spatial data}.
\section*{Calculus Toolkit}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}lX@{}}\toprule
Primary technique & Interpolation, gradients and surfaces \\
Prerequisites & functions, series or interpolation, approximation error \\
Numerical extension & Ch. 3 (interpolation), Ch. 5 (function evaluation), Ch. 6 (special functions) \\
Analytical/numerical class & NUMERICAL-NATURAL \\
\bottomrule\end{tabularx}
\section*{Modeling Questions to Ask}\begin{itemize}
\item What data or series is being approximated?
\item Is the evaluation interpolation or extrapolation?
\item What smoothness or convergence assumptions are used?
\item How will approximation quality be checked?
\end{itemize}
\begin{errorbox}Interpolation is not unrestricted extrapolation. State the interval on which the approximation is intended to be used and check approximation error when possible.\end{errorbox}
\section*{Problems}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-037-F-01} — Foundation}
\begin{problembox}
Two measurements in topographic maps, terrain and scattered spatial data are $(0,22)$ and $(10,37)$. Estimate the value at $x=4$ by linear interpolation.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What data or series is being approximated?
\item Is the evaluation interpolation or extrapolation?
\item What smoothness or convergence assumptions are used?
\item How will approximation quality be checked?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Estimate a value between two tabulated measurements.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par Let $y(x)$ denote the measured response.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par Use the line through the two data points.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Linear interpolation is appropriate because the requested $x$ lies inside the data interval.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $y(4)=22+\frac{15}{10}(4)$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $y(4)=28.000$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The estimate lies between 22 and 37, as expected for this monotone linear interpolant.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The interpolated value is approximately $28.000$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par Interpolation uses neighboring data; it does not claim that the same linear trend remains valid outside the interval.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-037-S-01} — Standard}
\begin{problembox}
A repeated contribution in topographic maps, terrain and scattered spatial data has magnitudes $285, 185.2, 120.4,\ldots$, with common ratio $r=0.65$. Determine the infinite accumulated total and state the convergence condition used.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What data or series is being approximated?
\item Is the evaluation interpolation or extrapolation?
\item What smoothness or convergence assumptions are used?
\item How will approximation quality be checked?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par The repeated contributions form a geometric series.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $a=285$ and $r=0.65$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $S=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}ar^n$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use the geometric-series sum because $|r|<1$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $S=a/(1-r)$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $S=285/(1-0.65)=814.286$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par $|r|<1$, so the infinite series converges.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The total accumulated contribution is $814.286$ units.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The convergence test must be checked before applying the geometric-series formula.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-037-A-01} — Advanced}
\begin{problembox}
A local approximation useful in topographic maps, terrain and scattered spatial data requires $\ln(1+x)$ at $x=0.2$. Use the cubic Taylor polynomial $x-x^2/2+x^3/3$, compare with the exact value, and report the absolute error.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What data or series is being approximated?
\item Is the evaluation interpolation or extrapolation?
\item What smoothness or convergence assumptions are used?
\item How will approximation quality be checked?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Approximate a transcendental value by a truncated power series.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $x=0.2$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $T_3(x)=x-x^2/2+x^3/3$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use a Taylor/power-series approximation near the expansion point $x=0$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $T_3(0.2)=0.2-0.2^2/2+0.2^3/3$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $T_3(0.2)\approx 0.18266667$, while $\ln(1.2)\approx 0.18232156$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The absolute error is about $3.45e-04$.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The cubic approximation is $0.18266667$ with absolute error about $3.45e-04$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par A local series converts a difficult function evaluation into polynomial arithmetic, with a measurable truncation error.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\begin{reflectionbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which sentence or datum in these problems supplied the main mathematical constraint?
\item Which assumptions were essential to the model, and how would the model change if one were removed?
\item When would an analytical solution become inconvenient enough to justify a numerical method?
\end{enumerate}\end{reflectionbox}

\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}

\item Is the evaluation interpolation or extrapolation?
\item What smoothness or convergence assumptions are used?
\item How will approximation quality be checked?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Approximate a transcendental value by a truncated power series.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $x=0.2$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $T_3(x)=x-x^2/2+x^3/3$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use a Taylor/power-series approximation near the expansion point $x=0$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $T_3(0.2)=0.2-0.2^2/2+0.2^3/3$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $T_3(0.2)\approx 0.18266667$, while $\ln(1.2)\approx 0.18232156$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The absolute error is about $3.45e-04$.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The cubic approximation is $0.18266667$ with absolute error about $3.45e-04$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par A local series converts a difficult function evaluation into polynomial arithmetic, with a measurable truncation error.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\begin{reflectionbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which sentence or datum in these problems supplied the main mathematical constraint?
\item Which assumptions were essential to the model, and how would the model change if one were removed?

\chapter{Tractrix and implicit/transcendental curve models}\label{app:A039}
\textbf{Application ID:} \pid{A039}\quad \textbf{Source locus:} 5; 8; 10\quad \textbf{Source pages:} 323, 395, 399, 549, 718
\section*{Application Context}
This family uses interpolation, series, transformations, and approximation to evaluate functions or represent data when direct formulas are inconvenient. The canonical application family is \emph{Tractrix and implicit/transcendental curve models}.
\section*{Calculus Toolkit}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}lX@{}}\toprule
Primary technique & Implicit curves, special functions and quadrature \\
Prerequisites & functions, series or interpolation, approximation error \\
Numerical extension & Ch. 3 (interpolation), Ch. 5 (function evaluation), Ch. 6 (special functions) \\
Analytical/numerical class & NUMERICAL-NATURAL \\
\bottomrule\end{tabularx}
\section*{Modeling Questions to Ask}\begin{itemize}
\item What data or series is being approximated?
\item Is the evaluation interpolation or extrapolation?
\item What smoothness or convergence assumptions are used?
\item How will approximation quality be checked?
\end{itemize}
\begin{errorbox}Interpolation is not unrestricted extrapolation. State the interval on which the approximation is intended to be used and check approximation error when possible.\end{errorbox}
\section*{Problems}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-039-F-01} — Foundation}
\begin{problembox}
Two measurements in tractrix and implicit/transcendental curve models are $(0,24)$ and $(10,39)$. Estimate the value at $x=4$ by linear interpolation.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What data or series is being approximated?
\item Is the evaluation interpolation or extrapolation?
\item What smoothness or convergence assumptions are used?
\item How will approximation quality be checked?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Estimate a value between two tabulated measurements.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par Let $y(x)$ denote the measured response.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par Use the line through the two data points.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Linear interpolation is appropriate because the requested $x$ lies inside the data interval.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $y(4)=24+\frac{15}{10}(4)$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $y(4)=30.000$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The estimate lies between 24 and 39, as expected for this monotone linear interpolant.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The interpolated value is approximately $30.000$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par Interpolation uses neighboring data; it does not claim that the same linear trend remains valid outside the interval.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-039-S-01} — Standard}
\begin{problembox}
A repeated contribution in tractrix and implicit/transcendental curve models has magnitudes $295, 177.0, 106.2,\ldots$, with common ratio $r=0.60$. Determine the infinite accumulated total and state the convergence condition used.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What data or series is being approximated?
\item Is the evaluation interpolation or extrapolation?
\item What smoothness or convergence assumptions are used?
\item How will approximation quality be checked?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par The repeated contributions form a geometric series.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $a=295$ and $r=0.60$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $S=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}ar^n$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use the geometric-series sum because $|r|<1$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $S=a/(1-r)$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $S=295/(1-0.60)=737.500$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par $|r|<1$, so the infinite series converges.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The total accumulated contribution is $737.500$ units.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The convergence test must be checked before applying the geometric-series formula.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-039-A-01} — Advanced}
\begin{problembox}
A local approximation useful in tractrix and implicit/transcendental curve models requires $\ln(1+x)$ at $x=0.2$. Use the cubic Taylor polynomial $x-x^2/2+x^3/3$, compare with the exact value, and report the absolute error.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What data or series is being approximated?
\item Is the evaluation interpolation or extrapolation?
\item What smoothness or convergence assumptions are used?
\item How will approximation quality be checked?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}

\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use a Taylor/power-series approximation near the expansion point $x=0$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $T_3(0.2)=0.2-0.2^2/2+0.2^3/3$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $T_3(0.2)\approx 0.18266667$, while $\ln(1.2)\approx 0.18232156$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The absolute error is about $3.45e-04$.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The cubic approximation is $0.18266667$ with absolute error about $3.45e-04$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par A local series converts a difficult function evaluation into polynomial arithmetic, with a measurable truncation error.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}

\item Which sentence or datum in these problems supplied the main mathematical constraint?
\item Which assumptions were essential to the model, and how would the model change if one were removed?
\item When would an analytical solution become inconvenient enough to justify a numerical method?

\section*{Problems}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-043-F-01} — Foundation}
\begin{problembox}
Two measurements in special-function physics: pendulum refinements, elliptic geometry and spherical harmonics are $(0,21)$ and $(10,36)$. Estimate the value at $x=4$ by linear interpolation.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What data or series is being approximated?
\item Is the evaluation interpolation or extrapolation?
\item What smoothness or convergence assumptions are used?
\item How will approximation quality be checked?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Estimate a value between two tabulated measurements.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par Let $y(x)$ denote the measured response.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par Use the line through the two data points.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Linear interpolation is appropriate because the requested $x$ lies inside the data interval.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $y(4)=21+\frac{15}{10}(4)$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $y(4)=27.000$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The estimate lies between 21 and 36, as expected for this monotone linear interpolant.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The interpolated value is approximately $27.000$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par Interpolation uses neighboring data; it does not claim that the same linear trend remains valid outside the interval.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-043-S-01} — Standard}
\begin{problembox}
A repeated contribution in special-function physics: pendulum refinements, elliptic geometry and spherical harmonics has magnitudes $315, 204.8, 133.1,\ldots$, with common ratio $r=0.65$. Determine the infinite accumulated total and state the convergence condition used.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What data or series is being approximated?
\item Is the evaluation interpolation or extrapolation?
\item What smoothness or convergence assumptions are used?
\item How will approximation quality be checked?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par The repeated contributions form a geometric series.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $a=315$ and $r=0.65$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $S=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}ar^n$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use the geometric-series sum because $|r|<1$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $S=a/(1-r)$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $S=315/(1-0.65)=900.000$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par $|r|<1$, so the infinite series converges.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The total accumulated contribution is $900.000$ units.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The convergence test must be checked before applying the geometric-series formula.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-043-A-01} — Advanced}
\begin{problembox}
A local approximation useful in special-function physics: pendulum refinements, elliptic geometry and spherical harmonics requires $\ln(1+x)$ at $x=0.2$. Use the cubic Taylor polynomial $x-x^2/2+x^3/3$, compare with the exact value, and report the absolute error.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What data or series is being approximated?
\item Is the evaluation interpolation or extrapolation?
\item What smoothness or convergence assumptions are used?
\item How will approximation quality be checked?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Approximate a transcendental value by a truncated power series.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $x=0.2$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $T_3(x)=x-x^2/2+x^3/3$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use a Taylor/power-series approximation near the expansion point $x=0$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $T_3(0.2)=0.2-0.2^2/2+0.2^3/3$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $T_3(0.2)\approx 0.18266667$, while $\ln(1.2)\approx 0.18232156$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The absolute error is about $3.45e-04$.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The cubic approximation is $0.18266667$ with absolute error about $3.45e-04$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par A local series converts a difficult function evaluation into polynomial arithmetic, with a measurable truncation error.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\begin{reflectionbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which sentence or datum in these problems supplied the main mathematical constraint?
\item Which assumptions were essential to the model, and how would the model change if one were removed?
\item When would an analytical solution become inconvenient enough to justify a numerical method?
\end{enumerate}\end{reflectionbox}
\part{Vectors and Multivariable Applications}
\chapter{Resultant force/velocity and vector mechanics}\label{app:A044}
\textbf{Application ID:} \pid{A044}\quad \textbf{Source locus:} 11.1–11.2\quad \textbf{Source pages:} 758–761
\section*{Application Context}
This family models directional and spatial quantities using vectors, gradients, linear systems, and multiple integrals. The canonical application family is \emph{Resultant force/velocity and vector mechanics}.
\section*{Calculus Toolkit}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}lX@{}}\toprule
Primary technique & Vector addition and components \\
Prerequisites & vectors, partial derivatives, linear algebra \\
Numerical extension & Ch. 2 (linear algebra), Ch. 10 (multivariable minimization), Ch. 11 (eigensystems) \\
Analytical/numerical class & NUMERICAL-OPTIONAL \\
\bottomrule\end{tabularx}
\section*{Modeling Questions to Ask}\begin{itemize}
\item Which quantities are scalars and which are vectors?
\item What coordinate system is being used?
\item Is a dot product, cross product, gradient, or multiple integral appropriate?
\item What geometric meaning should the result have?
\end{itemize}
\begin{errorbox}Keep scalar, vector, and coordinate quantities distinct. For gradients, state the point and normalize a direction vector before taking a directional derivative.\end{errorbox}
\section*{Problems}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-044-F-01} — Foundation}
\begin{problembox}
Two vector quantities in resultant force/velocity and vector mechanics are $\mathbf a=(4,-1,3)$ and $\mathbf b=(1,2,-2)$. Compute $\mathbf a\cdot\mathbf b$ and $\mathbf a\times\mathbf b$, and state one geometric interpretation of each.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which quantities are scalars and which are vectors?
\item What coordinate system is being used?
\item Is a dot product, cross product, gradient, or multiple integral appropriate?
\item What geometric meaning should the result have?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Compute the two fundamental bilinear vector operations.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $\mathbf a,\mathbf b\in\mathbb R^3$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $\mathbf a\cdot\mathbf b=\sum a_ib_i$ and $\mathbf a\times\mathbf b$ is the determinant-based cross product.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use vector algebra, the prerequisite language for many vector-calculus applications.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $\mathbf a\cdot\mathbf b=-4$ and $\mathbf a\times\mathbf b=(-4,11,9)$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par All required components are already numerical.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The cross product is perpendicular to both input vectors; dotting it with either vector gives zero.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par Dot product: $-4$. Cross product: $(-4,11,9)$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The dot product measures aligned component; the cross product encodes an oriented normal and parallelogram area.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-044-S-01} — Standard}
\begin{problembox}
A scalar field used in resultant force/velocity and vector mechanics is $F(x,y,z)=x^2+2y^2+3z$. At $P=(3,2,1)$, find the gradient and the directional derivative toward the direction $(1,2,1)$.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which quantities are scalars and which are vectors?
\item What coordinate system is being used?
\item Is a dot product, cross product, gradient, or multiple integral appropriate?
\item What geometric meaning should the result have?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par The gradient describes the direction of steepest increase; its dot product with a unit direction gives the directional derivative.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $F:\mathbb R^3\to\mathbb R$ and $\mathbf u=(1,2,1)/\sqrt6$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $\nabla F=(2x,4y,3)$ and $D_{\mathbf u}F=\nabla F\cdot\mathbf u$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use partial derivatives followed by a normalized-direction dot product.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $\nabla F(P)=(6,8,3)$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $D_{\mathbf u}F(P)=(6+2(8)+3)/\sqrt6\approx 10.2062$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The direction vector is normalized: $\|(1,2,1)/\sqrt6\|=1$.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par $\nabla F(P)=(6,8,3)$ and $D_{\mathbf u}F(P)\approx 10.2062$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par A directional derivative requires a unit vector; using an unnormalized direction would scale the rate incorrectly.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-044-A-01} — Advanced}
\begin{problembox}
A planar density model related to resultant force/velocity and vector mechanics is $\rho(x,y)=x+y$ on the rectangle $0\le x\le 4$, $0\le y\le 3$. Compute the total mass.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which quantities are scalars and which are vectors?
\item What coordinate system is being used?
\item Is a dot product, cross product, gradient, or multiple integral appropriate?
\item What geometric meaning should the result have?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Total mass is the double integral of area density.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $\rho$ has mass per area units; $dA=dy\,dx$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $M=\int_0^{4}\int_0^{3}(x+y)\,dy\,dx$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use an iterated double integral over the rectangular region.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $M=\int_0^{4}[3x+4.5]\,dx$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $M=42.000$ mass units.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The density is nonnegative on the region, so the positive total is consistent.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The total mass is $42.000$ units.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The model starts by identifying the density as the infinitesimal mass per unit area.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\begin{reflectionbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which sentence or datum in these problems supplied the main mathematical constraint?
\item Which assumptions were essential to the model, and how would the model change if one were removed?
\item When would an analytical solution become inconvenient enough to justify a numerical method?
\end{enumerate}\end{reflectionbox}

Two vector quantities in ideal-gas, meteorology and apparent-temperature multivariable models are $\mathbf a=(4,-2,3)$ and $\mathbf b=(1,5,-2)$. Compute $\mathbf a\cdot\mathbf b$ and $\mathbf a\times\mathbf b$, and state one geometric interpretation of each.

\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $\mathbf a\cdot\mathbf b=-12$ and $\mathbf a\times\mathbf b=(-11,11,22)$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par All required components are already numerical.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The cross product is perpendicular to both input vectors; dotting it with either vector gives zero.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par Dot product: $-12$. Cross product: $(-11,11,22)$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The dot product measures aligned component; the cross product encodes an oriented normal and parallelogram area.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-047-S-01} — Standard}
\begin{problembox}
A scalar field used in ideal-gas, meteorology and apparent-temperature multivariable models is $F(x,y,z)=x^2+2y^2+3z$. At $P=(3,2,1)$, find the gradient and the directional derivative toward the direction $(1,2,1)$.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which quantities are scalars and which are vectors?
\item What coordinate system is being used?
\item Is a dot product, cross product, gradient, or multiple integral appropriate?
\item What geometric meaning should the result have?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par The gradient describes the direction of steepest increase; its dot product with a unit direction gives the directional derivative.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $F:\mathbb R^3\to\mathbb R$ and $\mathbf u=(1,2,1)/\sqrt6$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $\nabla F=(2x,4y,3)$ and $D_{\mathbf u}F=\nabla F\cdot\mathbf u$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use partial derivatives followed by a normalized-direction dot product.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $\nabla F(P)=(6,8,3)$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $D_{\mathbf u}F(P)=(6+2(8)+3)/\sqrt6\approx 10.2062$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The direction vector is normalized: $\|(1,2,1)/\sqrt6\|=1$.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par $\nabla F(P)=(6,8,3)$ and $D_{\mathbf u}F(P)\approx 10.2062$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par A directional derivative requires a unit vector; using an unnormalized direction would scale the rate incorrectly.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-047-A-01} — Advanced}
\begin{problembox}
A planar density model related to ideal-gas, meteorology and apparent-temperature multivariable models is $\rho(x,y)=x+y$ on the rectangle $0\le x\le 4$, $0\le y\le 4$. Compute the total mass.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which quantities are scalars and which are vectors?
\item What coordinate system is being used?
\item Is a dot product, cross product, gradient, or multiple integral appropriate?
\item What geometric meaning should the result have?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Total mass is the double integral of area density.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $\rho$ has mass per area units; $dA=dy\,dx$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $M=\int_0^{4}\int_0^{4}(x+y)\,dy\,dx$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use an iterated double integral over the rectangular region.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $M=\int_0^{4}[4x+8.0]\,dx$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $M=64.000$ mass units.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The density is nonnegative on the region, so the positive total is consistent.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The total mass is $64.000$ units.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The model starts by identifying the density as the infinitesimal mass per unit area.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\begin{reflectionbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which sentence or datum in these problems supplied the main mathematical constraint?
\item Which assumptions were essential to the model, and how would the model change if one were removed?
\item When would an analytical solution become inconvenient enough to justify a numerical method?
\end{enumerate}\end{reflectionbox}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-048-F-01} — Foundation}
\begin{problembox}
Two vector quantities in cobb–douglas production, marginal analysis and economic surfaces are $\mathbf a=(2,-1,3)$ and $\mathbf b=(1,2,-2)$. Compute $\mathbf a\cdot\mathbf b$ and $\mathbf a\times\mathbf b$, and state one geometric interpretation of each.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which quantities are scalars and which are vectors?
\item What coordinate system is being used?
\item Is a dot product, cross product, gradient, or multiple integral appropriate?
\item What geometric meaning should the result have?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Compute the two fundamental bilinear vector operations.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $\mathbf a,\mathbf b\in\mathbb R^3$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $\mathbf a\cdot\mathbf b=\sum a_ib_i$ and $\mathbf a\times\mathbf b$ is the determinant-based cross product.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use vector algebra, the prerequisite language for many vector-calculus applications.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $\mathbf a\cdot\mathbf b=-6$ and $\mathbf a\times\mathbf b=(-4,7,5)$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par All required components are already numerical.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The cross product is perpendicular to both input vectors; dotting it with either vector gives zero.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par Dot product: $-6$. Cross product: $(-4,7,5)$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The dot product measures aligned component; the cross product encodes an oriented normal and parallelogram area.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}

\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which quantities are scalars and which are vectors?
\item What coordinate system is being used?
\item Is a dot product, cross product, gradient, or multiple integral appropriate?
\item What geometric meaning should the result have?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par The gradient describes the direction of steepest increase; its dot product with a unit direction gives the directional derivative.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $F:\mathbb R^3\to\mathbb R$ and $\mathbf u=(1,2,1)/\sqrt6$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $\nabla F=(2x,4y,3)$ and $D_{\mathbf u}F=\nabla F\cdot\mathbf u$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use partial derivatives followed by a normalized-direction dot product.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $\nabla F(P)=(2,8,3)$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $D_{\mathbf u}F(P)=(2+2(8)+3)/\sqrt6\approx 8.5732$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The direction vector is normalized: $\|(1,2,1)/\sqrt6\|=1$.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par $\nabla F(P)=(2,8,3)$ and $D_{\mathbf u}F(P)\approx 8.5732$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par A directional derivative requires a unit vector; using an unnormalized direction would scale the rate incorrectly.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-051-A-01} — Advanced}
\begin{problembox}
A planar density model related to field strength, electric potential and gravitational potential is $\rho(x,y)=x+y$ on the rectangle $0\le x\le 2$, $0\le y\le 4$. Compute the total mass.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which quantities are scalars and which are vectors?
\item What coordinate system is being used?
\item Is a dot product, cross product, gradient, or multiple integral appropriate?
\item What geometric meaning should the result have?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Total mass is the double integral of area density.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $\rho$ has mass per area units; $dA=dy\,dx$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $M=\int_0^{2}\int_0^{4}(x+y)\,dy\,dx$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use an iterated double integral over the rectangular region.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $M=\int_0^{2}[4x+8.0]\,dx$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $M=24.000$ mass units.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The density is nonnegative on the region, so the positive total is consistent.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The total mass is $24.000$ units.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The model starts by identifying the density as the infinitesimal mass per unit area.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\begin{reflectionbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which sentence or datum in these problems supplied the main mathematical constraint?
\item Which assumptions were essential to the model, and how would the model change if one were removed?
\item When would an analytical solution become inconvenient enough to justify a numerical method?
\end{enumerate}\end{reflectionbox}
\part{Differential Equations}
\chapter{Radioactive decay and carbon dating}\label{app:A052}
\textbf{Application ID:} \pid{A052}\quad \textbf{Source locus:} 5.5; 6.2–6.3\quad \textbf{Source pages:} 352, 417, 421, 429, 439
\section*{Application Context}
This family models a state whose derivative is governed by the state itself, external inputs, or coupled physical laws. Initial conditions select a unique trajectory when the model permits one. The canonical application family is \emph{Radioactive decay and carbon dating}.
\section*{Calculus Toolkit}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}lX@{}}\toprule
Primary technique & Exponential decay ODE \\
Prerequisites & derivatives, separation or linear ODE methods, initial conditions \\
Numerical extension & Ch. 17 (initial-value ODEs); Ch. 18 for two-point boundary values \\
Analytical/numerical class & NUMERICAL-OPTIONAL \\
\bottomrule\end{tabularx}
\section*{Modeling Questions to Ask}\begin{itemize}
\item What is the state variable?
\item What law specifies its derivative?
\item What initial/boundary data are available?
\item Can the solution be checked by substitution?
\end{itemize}
\begin{errorbox}An ODE solution is not determined until initial or boundary data are applied. Verify the solution by substitution and check the initial condition.\end{errorbox}
\section*{Problems}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-052-F-01} — Foundation}
\begin{problembox}
A state variable in radioactive decay and carbon dating satisfies $y'=0.08y$ with $y(0)=70$. Solve the initial-value problem and find $y(6)$.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What is the state variable?
\item What law specifies its derivative?
\item What initial/boundary data are available?
\item Can the solution be checked by substitution?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par The rate is proportional to the current state, producing exponential growth.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $y(t)$ is the state and $t$ is time.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $y'=0.08y$, $y(0)=70$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Separate variables or use the standard exponential-growth solution.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $y(t)=Ce^{0.08t}$ and the initial condition gives $C=70$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $y(6)=70e^{0.08(6)}\approx 113.125$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par Differentiating the solution returns $y'=ry$, and $y(0)$ matches the initial condition.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par $y(6)\approx 113.125$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The initial condition selects one trajectory from the family of exponential solutions.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-052-S-01} — Standard}
\begin{problembox}
A bounded-growth model for radioactive decay and carbon dating satisfies $y'=0.32y(1-y/540)$ with $y(0)=40$. Use the logistic solution to estimate $y(8)$.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What is the state variable?
\item What law specifies its derivative?
\item What initial/boundary data are available?
\item Can the solution be checked by substitution?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Growth slows as the state approaches a carrying capacity.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $K=540$ is carrying capacity, $r=0.32$ is intrinsic rate, and $y_0=40$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $y(t)=K/(1+A e^{-rt})$ with $A=(K-y_0)/y_0$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use the separable logistic ODE solution and the initial condition.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $A=(540-40)/40=12.50000$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $y(8)\approx 274.626$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par $0<y(8)<540$, consistent with a logistic state below carrying capacity.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The model predicts $y(8)\approx 274.626$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The carrying capacity changes the long-run behavior from unbounded exponential growth to saturation.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-052-A-01} — Advanced}
\begin{problembox}
A numerical extension of radioactive decay and carbon dating uses $y'=-0.5y+10$, $y(0)=0$. Apply Euler's method with step $h=0.5$ to approximate $y(2)$, then compare with the exact solution $y=20(1-e^{-0.5t})$.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What is the state variable?
\item What law specifies its derivative?
\item What initial/boundary data are available?
\item Can the solution be checked by substitution?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Approximate an IVP numerically and compare with a known exact solution.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $f(t,y)=-0.5y+10$, $h=0.5$, $y_0=0$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par Euler update: $y_{n+1}=y_n+h f(t_n,y_n)$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use forward Euler as a first-order numerical ODE method.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par Apply four updates to advance from $t=0$ to $t=2$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $y_1=5.00000$, $y_2=8.75000$, $y_3=11.56250$, $y_4=13.67188$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par Exact $y(2)\approx 12.64241$, so the Euler error is about $1.02946$.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par Euler gives $y(2)\approx 13.67188$; the exact value is $12.64241$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par A numerical method approximates the differential law over finite steps; reducing the step generally reduces truncation error for a stable problem.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\begin{reflectionbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which sentence or datum in these problems supplied the main mathematical constraint?
\item Which assumptions were essential to the model, and how would the model change if one were removed?
\item When would an analytical solution become inconvenient enough to justify a numerical method?
\end{enumerate}\end{reflectionbox}

\textbf{Construct the model}\par Euler update: $y_{n+1}=y_n+h f(t_n,y_n)$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use forward Euler as a first-order numerical ODE method.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par Apply four updates to advance from $t=0$ to $t=2$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $y_1=5.00000$, $y_2=8.75000$, $y_3=11.56250$, $y_4=13.67188$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par Exact $y(2)\approx 12.64241$, so the Euler error is about $1.02946$.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par Euler gives $y(2)\approx 13.67188$; the exact value is $12.64241$.\par\medskip

\section*{Modeling Questions to Ask}\begin{itemize}
\item What is the state variable?
\item What law specifies its derivative?
\item What initial/boundary data are available?
\item Can the solution be checked by substitution?
\end{itemize}
\begin{errorbox}An ODE solution is not determined until initial or boundary data are applied. Verify the solution by substitution and check the initial condition.\end{errorbox}
\section*{Problems}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-056-F-01} — Foundation}
\begin{problembox}
A state variable in newton cooling, heat transfer and thermal transients satisfies $y'=0.08y$ with $y(0)=60$. Solve the initial-value problem and find $y(6)$.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What is the state variable?
\item What law specifies its derivative?
\item What initial/boundary data are available?
\item Can the solution be checked by substitution?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par The rate is proportional to the current state, producing exponential growth.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $y(t)$ is the state and $t$ is time.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $y'=0.08y$, $y(0)=60$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Separate variables or use the standard exponential-growth solution.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $y(t)=Ce^{0.08t}$ and the initial condition gives $C=60$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $y(6)=60e^{0.08(6)}\approx 96.964$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par Differentiating the solution returns $y'=ry$, and $y(0)$ matches the initial condition.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par $y(6)\approx 96.964$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The initial condition selects one trajectory from the family of exponential solutions.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-056-S-01} — Standard}
\begin{problembox}
A bounded-growth model for newton cooling, heat transfer and thermal transients satisfies $y'=0.34y(1-y/520)$ with $y(0)=40$. Use the logistic solution to estimate $y(8)$.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What is the state variable?
\item What law specifies its derivative?
\item What initial/boundary data are available?
\item Can the solution be checked by substitution?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Growth slows as the state approaches a carrying capacity.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $K=520$ is carrying capacity, $r=0.34$ is intrinsic rate, and $y_0=40$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $y(t)=K/(1+A e^{-rt})$ with $A=(K-y_0)/y_0$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use the separable logistic ODE solution and the initial condition.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $A=(520-40)/40=12.00000$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $y(8)\approx 290.422$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par $0<y(8)<520$, consistent with a logistic state below carrying capacity.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The model predicts $y(8)\approx 290.422$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The carrying capacity changes the long-run behavior from unbounded exponential growth to saturation.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
