\chapter{Falling bodies and velocity in a resisting medium}\label{app:A060}
\textbf{Application ID:} \pid{A060}\quad \textbf{Source locus:} 1; 6\quad \textbf{Source pages:} 73, 95, 311, 371, 434, 437
\section*{Application Context}
This family models a state whose derivative is governed by the state itself, external inputs, or coupled physical laws. Initial conditions select a unique trajectory when the model permits one. The canonical application family is \emph{Falling bodies and velocity in a resisting medium}.
\section*{Calculus Toolkit}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}lX@{}}\toprule
Primary technique & Initial-value ODEs with drag \\
Prerequisites & derivatives, separation or linear ODE methods, initial conditions \\
Numerical extension & Ch. 17 (initial-value ODEs); Ch. 18 for two-point boundary values \\
Analytical/numerical class & NUMERICAL-NATURAL \\
\bottomrule\end{tabularx}
\section*{Modeling Questions to Ask}\begin{itemize}
\item What is the state variable?
\item What law specifies its derivative?
\item What initial/boundary data are available?
\item Can the solution be checked by substitution?
\end{itemize}
\begin{errorbox}An ODE solution is not determined until initial or boundary data are applied. Verify the solution by substitution and check the initial condition.\end{errorbox}
\section*{Problems}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-060-F-01} — Foundation}
\begin{problembox}
A state variable in falling bodies and velocity in a resisting medium satisfies $y'=0.08y$ with $y(0)=50$. Solve the initial-value problem and find $y(6)$.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What is the state variable?
\item What law specifies its derivative?
\item What initial/boundary data are available?
\item Can the solution be checked by substitution?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par The rate is proportional to the current state, producing exponential growth.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $y(t)$ is the state and $t$ is time.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $y'=0.08y$, $y(0)=50$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Separate variables or use the standard exponential-growth solution.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $y(t)=Ce^{0.08t}$ and the initial condition gives $C=50$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $y(6)=50e^{0.08(6)}\approx 80.804$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par Differentiating the solution returns $y'=ry$, and $y(0)$ matches the initial condition.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par $y(6)\approx 80.804$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The initial condition selects one trajectory from the family of exponential solutions.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-060-S-01} — Standard}
\begin{problembox}
A bounded-growth model for falling bodies and velocity in a resisting medium satisfies $y'=0.30y(1-y/500)$ with $y(0)=40$. Use the logistic solution to estimate $y(8)$.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What is the state variable?
\item What law specifies its derivative?
\item What initial/boundary data are available?
\item Can the solution be checked by substitution?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Growth slows as the state approaches a carrying capacity.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $K=500$ is carrying capacity, $r=0.30$ is intrinsic rate, and $y_0=40$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $y(t)=K/(1+A e^{-rt})$ with $A=(K-y_0)/y_0$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use the separable logistic ODE solution and the initial condition.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $A=(500-40)/40=11.50000$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $y(8)\approx 244.707$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par $0<y(8)<500$, consistent with a logistic state below carrying capacity.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The model predicts $y(8)\approx 244.707$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The carrying capacity changes the long-run behavior from unbounded exponential growth to saturation.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-060-A-01} — Advanced}
\begin{problembox}
A numerical extension of falling bodies and velocity in a resisting medium uses $y'=-0.5y+10$, $y(0)=0$. Apply Euler's method with step $h=0.5$ to approximate $y(2)$, then compare with the exact solution $y=20(1-e^{-0.5t})$.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What is the state variable?
\item What law specifies its derivative?
\item What initial/boundary data are available?
\item Can the solution be checked by substitution?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Approximate an IVP numerically and compare with a known exact solution.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $f(t,y)=-0.5y+10$, $h=0.5$, $y_0=0$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par Euler update: $y_{n+1}=y_n+h f(t_n,y_n)$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use forward Euler as a first-order numerical ODE method.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par Apply four updates to advance from $t=0$ to $t=2$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $y_1=5.00000$, $y_2=8.75000$, $y_3=11.56250$, $y_4=13.67188$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par Exact $y(2)\approx 12.64241$, so the Euler error is about $1.02946$.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par Euler gives $y(2)\approx 13.67188$; the exact value is $12.64241$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par A numerical method approximates the differential law over finite steps; reducing the step generally reduces truncation error for a stable problem.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\begin{reflectionbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which sentence or datum in these problems supplied the main mathematical constraint?
\item Which assumptions were essential to the model, and how would the model change if one were removed?
\item When would an analytical solution become inconvenient enough to justify a numerical method?
\end{enumerate}\end{reflectionbox}

\chapter{Sales, inflation, health-care expenditures, government expenditures and GDP data}\label{app:A064}
\textbf{Application ID:} \pid{A064}\quad \textbf{Source locus:} P; 5; 13\quad \textbf{Source pages:} 9, 131, 359, 597, 883, 903
\section*{Application Context}
This family begins with measured or simulated data and uses statistics, regression, probability, or Monte Carlo techniques to quantify patterns and uncertainty. The canonical application family is \emph{Sales, inflation, health-care expenditures, government expenditures and GDP data}.
\section*{Calculus Toolkit}

Primary technique & Regression, trend estimation and uncertainty \\

\section*{Modeling Questions to Ask}\begin{itemize}
\item What are the observations and response variable?
\item What model class is being assumed?
\item What statistic or fitted parameter is requested?
\item How should residuals or uncertainty be interpreted?
\end{itemize}
\begin{errorbox}Do not treat a fitted relationship as an exact law. Distinguish observations, model predictions, residuals, and uncertainty.\end{errorbox}
\section*{Problems}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-064-F-01} — Foundation}
\begin{problembox}
Five observations in sales, inflation, health-care expenditures, government expenditures and gdp data are [14, 16, 19, 17, 24]. Compute the sample mean and sample variance.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What are the observations and response variable?
\item What model class is being assumed?
\item What statistic or fitted parameter is requested?
\item How should residuals or uncertainty be interpreted?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Summarize the center and spread of a small dataset.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $x_1,\dots,x_5$ are the observations.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $\bar x=\frac1n\sum x_i$ and $s^2=\frac1{n-1}\sum(x_i-\bar x)^2$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par This is a statistical summary supporting data-driven calculus models.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $\bar x=18.000$ and $s^2=14.500$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par The values above are inserted directly into the definitions.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The mean lies within the data range and the variance is nonnegative.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par Mean $=18.000$; sample variance $=14.500$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par Descriptive statistics characterize the observations before a functional model is fitted.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-064-S-01} — Standard}
\begin{problembox}
A small dataset for sales, inflation, health-care expenditures, government expenditures and gdp data has points $(0,6),(1,8),(2,10),(3,13)$. Find the least-squares line $y=mx+b$.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What are the observations and response variable?
\item What model class is being assumed?
\item What statistic or fitted parameter is requested?
\item How should residuals or uncertainty be interpreted?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Fit a line that minimizes the sum of squared vertical residuals.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $m$ is slope and $b$ intercept.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $m=S_{xy}/S_{xx}$ and $b=\bar y-m\bar x$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Least squares can be derived by minimizing a quadratic error function; use its normal-equation formulas here.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $\bar x=1.50$, $\bar y=9.25$, $S_{xx}=5.00$, $S_{xy}=11.50$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $m=2.3000$, $b=5.8000$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par Residuals should sum to zero when an intercept is included; this fitted line satisfies that property up to rounding.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par $\hat y=2.3000x+5.8000$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par Regression chooses parameters by an optimization criterion rather than forcing the line through every noisy observation.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-064-A-01} — Advanced}
\begin{problembox}
For a simple uncertainty exercise related to sales, inflation, health-care expenditures, government expenditures and gdp data, ten uniform samples on $[0,1]$ are [0.11, 0.27, 0.33, 0.48, 0.52, 0.61, 0.73, 0.81, 0.89, 0.95]. Estimate $P(X^2<0.5)$ by Monte Carlo frequency and compare with the exact probability.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What are the observations and response variable?
\item What model class is being assumed?
\item What statistic or fitted parameter is requested?
\item How should residuals or uncertainty be interpreted?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Estimate a probability from simulated uniform samples and compare with the analytical benchmark.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $X\sim U(0,1)$; the event is $X^2<0.5$, equivalently $X<\sqrt{0.5}$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $\widehat p=(\text{number of hits})/N$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use Monte Carlo frequency as a computational probability approximation.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par 6 of 10 samples satisfy the event, so $\widehat p=0.600$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par Exact $p=\sqrt{0.5}\approx 0.70711$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The sampling error for this small experiment is about $0.10711$.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par Monte Carlo estimate $=0.600$; exact probability $\approx 0.70711$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par Monte Carlo replaces integration by repeated sampling; accuracy improves statistically with more samples, not deterministically at a fixed rate.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\begin{reflectionbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which sentence or datum in these problems supplied the main mathematical constraint?
\item Which assumptions were essential to the model, and how would the model change if one were removed?
\item When would an analytical solution become inconvenient enough to justify a numerical method?
\end{enumerate}\end{reflectionbox}

\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Fit a line that minimizes the sum of squared vertical residuals.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $m$ is slope and $b$ intercept.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $m=S_{xy}/S_{xx}$ and $b=\bar y-m\bar x$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Least squares can be derived by minimizing a quadratic error function; use its normal-equation formulas here.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $\bar x=1.50$, $\bar y=10.25$, $S_{xx}=5.00$, $S_{xy}=11.50$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $m=2.3000$, $b=6.8000$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par Residuals should sum to zero when an intercept is included; this fitted line satisfies that property up to rounding.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par $\hat y=2.3000x+6.8000$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par Regression chooses parameters by an optimization criterion rather than forcing the line through every noisy observation.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-068-A-01} — Advanced}
\begin{problembox}
For a simple uncertainty exercise related to agronomy, forestry, biomass and ecological field data, ten uniform samples on $[0,1]$ are [0.11, 0.27, 0.33, 0.48, 0.52, 0.61, 0.73, 0.81, 0.89, 0.95]. Estimate $P(X^2<0.5)$ by Monte Carlo frequency and compare with the exact probability.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What are the observations and response variable?
\item What model class is being assumed?
\item What statistic or fitted parameter is requested?
\item How should residuals or uncertainty be interpreted?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Estimate a probability from simulated uniform samples and compare with the analytical benchmark.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $X\sim U(0,1)$; the event is $X^2<0.5$, equivalently $X<\sqrt{0.5}$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $\widehat p=(\text{number of hits})/N$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use Monte Carlo frequency as a computational probability approximation.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par 6 of 10 samples satisfy the event, so $\widehat p=0.600$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par Exact $p=\sqrt{0.5}\approx 0.70711$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The sampling error for this small experiment is about $0.10711$.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par Monte Carlo estimate $=0.600$; exact probability $\approx 0.70711$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par Monte Carlo replaces integration by repeated sampling; accuracy improves statistically with more samples, not deterministically at a fixed rate.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\begin{reflectionbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which sentence or datum in these problems supplied the main mathematical constraint?
\item Which assumptions were essential to the model, and how would the model change if one were removed?
\item When would an analytical solution become inconvenient enough to justify a numerical method?
\end{enumerate}\end{reflectionbox}
\chapter{Probability simulation and uncertainty propagation}\label{app:A069}
\textbf{Application ID:} \pid{A069}\quad \textbf{Source locus:} 5–14\quad \textbf{Source pages:} 351, 581, 584, 606, 607, 667, 678, 989, 997
\section*{Application Context}
This family begins with measured or simulated data and uses statistics, regression, probability, or Monte Carlo techniques to quantify patterns and uncertainty. The canonical application family is \emph{Probability simulation and uncertainty propagation}.
\section*{Calculus Toolkit}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}lX@{}}\toprule
Primary technique & Random variates and Monte Carlo \\
Prerequisites & functions, descriptive statistics, model fitting \\
Numerical extension & Ch. 14 (statistics), Ch. 15 (modeling), Ch. 7 (random numbers) \\
Analytical/numerical class & NUMERICAL-NATURAL \\
\bottomrule\end{tabularx}
\section*{Modeling Questions to Ask}\begin{itemize}
\item What are the observations and response variable?
\item What model class is being assumed?
\item What statistic or fitted parameter is requested?
\item How should residuals or uncertainty be interpreted?
\end{itemize}
\begin{errorbox}Do not treat a fitted relationship as an exact law. Distinguish observations, model predictions, residuals, and uncertainty.\end{errorbox}
\section*{Problems}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-069-F-01} — Foundation}
\begin{problembox}
Five observations in probability simulation and uncertainty propagation are [14, 16, 19, 17, 24]. Compute the sample mean and sample variance.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What are the observations and response variable?
\item What model class is being assumed?
\item What statistic or fitted parameter is requested?
\item How should residuals or uncertainty be interpreted?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Summarize the center and spread of a small dataset.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $x_1,\dots,x_5$ are the observations.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $\bar x=\frac1n\sum x_i$ and $s^2=\frac1{n-1}\sum(x_i-\bar x)^2$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par This is a statistical summary supporting data-driven calculus models.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $\bar x=18.000$ and $s^2=14.500$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par The values above are inserted directly into the definitions.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The mean lies within the data range and the variance is nonnegative.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par Mean $=18.000$; sample variance $=14.500$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par Descriptive statistics characterize the observations before a functional model is fitted.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-069-S-01} — Standard}
\begin{problembox}
A small dataset for probability simulation and uncertainty propagation has points $(0,5),(1,7),(2,9),(3,12)$. Find the least-squares line $y=mx+b$.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What are the observations and response variable?
\item What model class is being assumed?
\item What statistic or fitted parameter is requested?
\item How should residuals or uncertainty be interpreted?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Fit a line that minimizes the sum of squared vertical residuals.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $m$ is slope and $b$ intercept.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $m=S_{xy}/S_{xx}$ and $b=\bar y-m\bar x$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Least squares can be derived by minimizing a quadratic error function; use its normal-equation formulas here.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $\bar x=1.50$, $\bar y=8.25$, $S_{xx}=5.00$, $S_{xy}=11.50$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $m=2.3000$, $b=4.8000$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par Residuals should sum to zero when an intercept is included; this fitted line satisfies that property up to rounding.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par $\hat y=2.3000x+4.8000$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par Regression chooses parameters by an optimization criterion rather than forcing the line through every noisy observation.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-069-A-01} — Advanced}

\begin{reflectionbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which sentence or datum in these problems supplied the main mathematical constraint?

\part{Spectral and Mode Applications}
\chapter{Vibrating string, wave motion and periodic mechanical signals}\label{app:A070}
\textbf{Application ID:} \pid{A070}\quad \textbf{Source locus:} 2; 7\quad \textbf{Source pages:} 142, 161, 531
\section*{Application Context}
This family represents periodic or modal behavior using sinusoidal components, Fourier coefficients, eigenvectors, or related transforms. The canonical application family is \emph{Vibrating string, wave motion and periodic mechanical signals}.
\section*{Calculus Toolkit}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}lX@{}}\toprule
Primary technique & Fourier/spectral decomposition \\
Prerequisites & trigonometric functions, complex/vector algebra as needed \\
Numerical extension & Ch. 12 (FFT), Ch. 13 (spectral applications) \\
Analytical/numerical class & NUMERICAL-NATURAL \\
\bottomrule\end{tabularx}
\section*{Modeling Questions to Ask}\begin{itemize}
\item What is the period or sampling interval?
\item Which frequencies can be represented?
\item What does a spectral coefficient mean physically?
\item How is the transform normalized?
\end{itemize}
\begin{errorbox}Frequency, angular frequency, period, and sampling index are different quantities. Keep their units and normalizations explicit.\end{errorbox}
\section*{Problems}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-070-F-01} — Foundation}
\begin{problembox}
A periodic signal associated with vibrating string, wave motion and periodic mechanical signals is $s(t)=4\cos(1.2566t)$. Find its period and ordinary frequency.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What is the period or sampling interval?
\item Which frequencies can be represented?
\item What does a spectral coefficient mean physically?
\item How is the transform normalized?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Extract period and cycles-per-time frequency from angular frequency.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $\omega$ is angular frequency in radians per time unit.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $T=2\pi/\omega$ and $f=1/T=\omega/(2\pi)$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use the periodic structure of sinusoidal functions, a prerequisite to Fourier analysis.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $T\approx 5.0000$ and $f\approx 0.2000$ cycles per time unit.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par The given angular frequency is inserted directly.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par $\omega T=2\pi$, as required for one full cycle.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par Period $T\approx 5.0000$; frequency $f\approx 0.2000$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par Angular frequency and ordinary frequency differ by a factor of $2\pi$.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-070-S-01} — Standard}
\begin{problembox}
A four-sample signal in vibrating string, wave motion and periodic mechanical signals is $x=[1,0,-1,0]$. Compute its unnormalized discrete Fourier transform $X_k=\sum_{n=0}^3 x_ne^{-2\pi i kn/4}$.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What is the period or sampling interval?
\item Which frequencies can be represented?
\item What does a spectral coefficient mean physically?
\item How is the transform normalized?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Transform four equally spaced samples from the time/sample domain to discrete frequency coefficients.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $N=4$ and $X_k=\sum_{n=0}^{3}x_n e^{-2\pi i kn/4}$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par Only $x_0=1$ and $x_2=-1$ are nonzero, so $X_k=1-e^{-\pi i k}=1-(-1)^k$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use the DFT definition, the finite-dimensional basis of FFT algorithms.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par For even $k$, $X_k=0$; for odd $k$, $X_k=2$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $X=[0,2,0,2]$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The transform is conjugate-symmetric because the input data are real.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The unnormalized DFT coefficients are $X_0=0,X_1=2,X_2=0,X_3=2$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par Sparse time-domain structure can yield a simple algebraic expression for every Fourier coefficient.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-070-A-01} — Advanced}
\begin{problembox}
A two-mode model for vibrating string, wave motion and periodic mechanical signals has system matrix $A=\begin{pmatrix}2&1\\1&2\end{pmatrix}$. Find its eigenvalues and eigenvectors and interpret them as independent modal directions.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What is the period or sampling interval?
\item Which frequencies can be represented?
\item What does a spectral coefficient mean physically?
\item How is the transform normalized?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Normal modes are eigenvectors of the system matrix.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par Solve $A\mathbf v=\lambda\mathbf v$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $\det(A-\lambda I)=(2-\lambda)^2-1=0$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use eigensystem analysis to decouple the two-mode linear system.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $(2-\lambda)^2=1$, so $\lambda=3,1$. For $\lambda=3$, $\mathbf v\propto(1,1)$; for $\lambda=1$, $\mathbf v\propto(1,-1)$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par The matrix entries are already numerical.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par $A(1,1)^T=3(1,1)^T$ and $A(1,-1)^T=(1,-1)^T$.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The modal pairs are $(\lambda_1,\mathbf v_1)=(3,(1,1))$ and $(\lambda_2,\mathbf v_2)=(1,(1,-1))$ up to scaling.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par Eigenvectors identify directions that evolve independently under a linear system.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\begin{reflectionbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which sentence or datum in these problems supplied the main mathematical constraint?
\item Which assumptions were essential to the model, and how would the model change if one were removed?
\item When would an analytical solution become inconvenient enough to justify a numerical method?
\end{enumerate}\end{reflectionbox}

\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Normal modes are eigenvectors of the system matrix.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par Solve $A\mathbf v=\lambda\mathbf v$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $\det(A-\lambda I)=(2-\lambda)^2-1=0$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use eigensystem analysis to decouple the two-mode linear system.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $(2-\lambda)^2=1$, so $\lambda=3,1$. For $\lambda=3$, $\mathbf v\propto(1,1)$; for $\lambda=1$, $\mathbf v\propto(1,-1)$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par The matrix entries are already numerical.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par $A(1,1)^T=3(1,1)^T$ and $A(1,-1)^T=(1,-1)^T$.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The modal pairs are $(\lambda_1,\mathbf v_1)=(3,(1,1))$ and $(\lambda_2,\mathbf v_2)=(1,(1,-1))$ up to scaling.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par Eigenvectors identify directions that evolve independently under a linear system.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\begin{reflectionbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which sentence or datum in these problems supplied the main mathematical constraint?
\item Which assumptions were essential to the model, and how would the model change if one were removed?
\item When would an analytical solution become inconvenient enough to justify a numerical method?
\end{enumerate}\end{reflectionbox}
\chapter{Multiscale or localized signal/image analysis}\label{app:A073}
\textbf{Application ID:} \pid{A073}\quad \textbf{Source locus:} 9; 13\quad \textbf{Source pages:} general extension
\section*{Application Context}
This family represents periodic or modal behavior using sinusoidal components, Fourier coefficients, eigenvectors, or related transforms. The canonical application family is \emph{Multiscale or localized signal/image analysis}.
\section*{Calculus Toolkit}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}lX@{}}\toprule
Primary technique & Wavelet transforms \\
Prerequisites & trigonometric functions, complex/vector algebra as needed \\
Numerical extension & Ch. 12 (FFT), Ch. 13 (spectral applications) \\
Analytical/numerical class & NUMERICAL-NATURAL \\
\bottomrule\end{tabularx}
\section*{Modeling Questions to Ask}\begin{itemize}
\item What is the period or sampling interval?
\item Which frequencies can be represented?
\item What does a spectral coefficient mean physically?
\item How is the transform normalized?
\end{itemize}
\begin{errorbox}Frequency, angular frequency, period, and sampling index are different quantities. Keep their units and normalizations explicit.\end{errorbox}
\section*{Problems}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-073-F-01} — Foundation}
\begin{problembox}
A periodic signal associated with multiscale or localized signal/image analysis is $s(t)=4\cos(1.2566t)$. Find its period and ordinary frequency.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What is the period or sampling interval?
\item Which frequencies can be represented?
\item What does a spectral coefficient mean physically?
\item How is the transform normalized?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Extract period and cycles-per-time frequency from angular frequency.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $\omega$ is angular frequency in radians per time unit.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $T=2\pi/\omega$ and $f=1/T=\omega/(2\pi)$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use the periodic structure of sinusoidal functions, a prerequisite to Fourier analysis.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $T\approx 5.0000$ and $f\approx 0.2000$ cycles per time unit.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par The given angular frequency is inserted directly.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par $\omega T=2\pi$, as required for one full cycle.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par Period $T\approx 5.0000$; frequency $f\approx 0.2000$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par Angular frequency and ordinary frequency differ by a factor of $2\pi$.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-073-S-01} — Standard}
\begin{problembox}
A four-sample signal in multiscale or localized signal/image analysis is $x=[1,0,-1,0]$. Compute its unnormalized discrete Fourier transform $X_k=\sum_{n=0}^3 x_ne^{-2\pi i kn/4}$.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What is the period or sampling interval?
\item Which frequencies can be represented?
\item What does a spectral coefficient mean physically?
\item How is the transform normalized?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Transform four equally spaced samples from the time/sample domain to discrete frequency coefficients.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $N=4$ and $X_k=\sum_{n=0}^{3}x_n e^{-2\pi i kn/4}$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par Only $x_0=1$ and $x_2=-1$ are nonzero, so $X_k=1-e^{-\pi i k}=1-(-1)^k$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use the DFT definition, the finite-dimensional basis of FFT algorithms.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par For even $k$, $X_k=0$; for odd $k$, $X_k=2$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $X=[0,2,0,2]$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The transform is conjugate-symmetric because the input data are real.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The unnormalized DFT coefficients are $X_0=0,X_1=2,X_2=0,X_3=2$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par Sparse time-domain structure can yield a simple algebraic expression for every Fourier coefficient.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-073-A-01} — Advanced}
\begin{problembox}
A two-mode model for multiscale or localized signal/image analysis has system matrix $A=\begin{pmatrix}2&1\\1&2\end{pmatrix}$. Find its eigenvalues and eigenvectors and interpret them as independent modal directions.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What is the period or sampling interval?
\item Which frequencies can be represented?
\item What does a spectral coefficient mean physically?
\item How is the transform normalized?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Normal modes are eigenvectors of the system matrix.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par Solve $A\mathbf v=\lambda\mathbf v$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $\det(A-\lambda I)=(2-\lambda)^2-1=0$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use eigensystem analysis to decouple the two-mode linear system.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $(2-\lambda)^2=1$, so $\lambda=3,1$. For $\lambda=3$, $\mathbf v\propto(1,1)$; for $\lambda=1$, $\mathbf v\propto(1,-1)$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par The matrix entries are already numerical.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par $A(1,1)^T=3(1,1)^T$ and $A(1,-1)^T=(1,-1)^T$.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The modal pairs are $(\lambda_1,\mathbf v_1)=(3,(1,1))$ and $(\lambda_2,\mathbf v_2)=(1,(1,-1))$ up to scaling.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par Eigenvectors identify directions that evolve independently under a linear system.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\begin{reflectionbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which sentence or datum in these problems supplied the main mathematical constraint?
\item Which assumptions were essential to the model, and how would the model change if one were removed?
\item When would an analytical solution become inconvenient enough to justify a numerical method?
\end{enumerate}\end{reflectionbox}
\part{Advanced Field, Boundary, and PDE Applications}
\chapter{Heat equation and temperature distribution}\label{app:A074}
\textbf{Application ID:} \pid{A074}\quad \textbf{Source locus:} 13; advanced DE\quad \textbf{Source pages:} 901, 902, 925, 930, 967
\section*{Application Context}
This family extends calculus to boundary-value, field, inverse, or PDE problems where discretization and linear algebra frequently become part of the solution. The canonical application family is \emph{Heat equation and temperature distribution}.
\section*{Calculus Toolkit}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}lX@{}}\toprule
Primary technique & Parabolic PDEs and boundary/initial conditions \\
Prerequisites & multivariable calculus, differential equations, linear algebra \\
Numerical extension & Chs. 18–21 (boundary values, inverse problems, PDEs, computational geometry) \\
Analytical/numerical class & NUMERICAL-REQUIRED \\
\bottomrule\end{tabularx}
\section*{Modeling Questions to Ask}\begin{itemize}
\item What PDE/BVP/field equation is being modeled?
\item What boundary and initial conditions are required?
\item What quantity is conserved or balanced?
\item Which part is analytical and which part requires discretization?
\end{itemize}
\begin{errorbox}Separate the mathematical model, boundary/initial conditions, and discretization. A numerically solvable scheme is not automatically stable or convergent.\end{errorbox}
\section*{Problems}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-074-F-01} — Foundation}
\begin{problembox}
For an advanced model related to heat equation and temperature distribution, verify that $u(x,t)=e^{-\pi^2t}\sin(\pi x)$ satisfies the heat equation $u_t=u_{xx}$ and the boundary conditions $u(0,t)=u(1,t)=0$.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What PDE/BVP/field equation is being modeled?
\item What boundary and initial conditions are required?
\item What quantity is conserved or balanced?
\item Which part is analytical and which part requires discretization?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Verification means differentiate the candidate and check both the PDE and boundary data.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $u$ is a scalar field on $0\le x\le1$, $t\ge0$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par Heat equation: $u_t=u_{xx}$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use partial differentiation and direct substitution.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $u_t=-\pi^2 e^{-\pi^2t}\sin(\pi x)$ and $u_{xx}=-\pi^2 e^{-\pi^2t}\sin(\pi x)$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $u(0,t)=0$ and $u(1,t)=e^{-\pi^2t}\sin\pi=0$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The PDE identity and both boundary conditions hold for all $t\ge0$.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The candidate is a valid solution of the stated heat-equation boundary-value problem.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par For PDE verification, the differential equation and every boundary/initial condition must be checked separately.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-074-S-01} — Standard}
\begin{problembox}
A finite-difference model for heat equation and temperature distribution has one interior grid value $u$ surrounded by boundary values 14, 22, 32, and 40. For the five-point Laplace stencil, compute the interior value.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What PDE/BVP/field equation is being modeled?
\item What boundary and initial conditions are required?
\item What quantity is conserved or balanced?
\item Which part is analytical and which part requires discretization?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par The discrete Laplace equation sets the center equal to the average of its four nearest neighbors.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $u$ is the unknown interior grid value.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $-4u+u_E+u_W+u_N+u_S=0$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use a finite-difference discretization of the Laplace operator.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $u=(u_E+u_W+u_N+u_S)/4$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $u=(14+22+32+40)/4=27.000$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par A discrete harmonic interior value lies between the minimum and maximum surrounding boundary values; it does.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The interior grid value is $u=27.000$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par Discretization converts a differential field equation into an algebraic balance equation.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-074-A-01} — Advanced}
\begin{problembox}
As a computational extension of heat equation and temperature distribution, solve $-u''=1$ on $0<x<1$ with $u(0)=u(1)=0$ using a centered finite difference on grid points $x=0,1/3,2/3,1$. Find the two interior unknowns and compare with the exact solution $u=x(1-x)/2$.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What PDE/BVP/field equation is being modeled?
\item What boundary and initial conditions are required?
\item What quantity is conserved or balanced?
\item Which part is analytical and which part requires discretization?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Discretize a two-point boundary-value problem into a small linear system.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $u_1\approx u(1/3)$ and $u_2\approx u(2/3)$; $h=1/3$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $-(u_{i-1}-2u_i+u_{i+1})/h^2=1$, hence $2u_i-u_{i-1}-u_{i+1}=h^2$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use centered finite differences with the boundary values inserted into the endpoint-adjacent equations.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $2u_1-u_2=h^2$ and $-u_1+2u_2=h^2$. Symmetry gives $u_1=u_2=h^2$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $u_1=u_2=1/9\approx 0.111111$. Exact $u(1/3)=u(2/3)=1/9\approx 0.111111$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par For this quadratic exact solution, the centered second difference reproduces the second derivative exactly.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par $u_1=u_2\approx 0.111111$, matching the exact values.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par A boundary-value differential equation becomes a sparse linear algebra problem after discretization.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\begin{reflectionbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which sentence or datum in these problems supplied the main mathematical constraint?
\item Which assumptions were essential to the model, and how would the model change if one were removed?
\item When would an analytical solution become inconvenient enough to justify a numerical method?
\end{enumerate}\end{reflectionbox}

\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use centered finite differences with the boundary values inserted into the endpoint-adjacent equations.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $2u_1-u_2=h^2$ and $-u_1+2u_2=h^2$. Symmetry gives $u_1=u_2=h^2$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $u_1=u_2=1/9\approx 0.111111$. Exact $u(1/3)=u(2/3)=1/9\approx 0.111111$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par For this quadratic exact solution, the centered second difference reproduces the second derivative exactly.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par $u_1=u_2\approx 0.111111$, matching the exact values.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par A boundary-value differential equation becomes a sparse linear algebra problem after discretization.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\begin{reflectionbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which sentence or datum in these problems supplied the main mathematical constraint?
\item Which assumptions were essential to the model, and how would the model change if one were removed?
\item When would an analytical solution become inconvenient enough to justify a numerical method?
\end{enumerate}\end{reflectionbox}

\section*{Modeling Questions to Ask}\begin{itemize}
\item What PDE/BVP/field equation is being modeled?
\item What boundary and initial conditions are required?
\item What quantity is conserved or balanced?
\item Which part is analytical and which part requires discretization?
\end{itemize}
\begin{errorbox}Separate the mathematical model, boundary/initial conditions, and discretization. A numerically solvable scheme is not automatically stable or convergent.\end{errorbox}
\section*{Problems}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-077-F-01} — Foundation}
\begin{problembox}
For an advanced model related to electric/gravitational fields, gauss's law, charge and heat flux, verify that $u(x,t)=e^{-\pi^2t}\sin(\pi x)$ satisfies the heat equation $u_t=u_{xx}$ and the boundary conditions $u(0,t)=u(1,t)=0$.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What PDE/BVP/field equation is being modeled?
\item What boundary and initial conditions are required?
\item What quantity is conserved or balanced?
\item Which part is analytical and which part requires discretization?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Verification means differentiate the candidate and check both the PDE and boundary data.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $u$ is a scalar field on $0\le x\le1$, $t\ge0$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par Heat equation: $u_t=u_{xx}$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use partial differentiation and direct substitution.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $u_t=-\pi^2 e^{-\pi^2t}\sin(\pi x)$ and $u_{xx}=-\pi^2 e^{-\pi^2t}\sin(\pi x)$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $u(0,t)=0$ and $u(1,t)=e^{-\pi^2t}\sin\pi=0$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The PDE identity and both boundary conditions hold for all $t\ge0$.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The candidate is a valid solution of the stated heat-equation boundary-value problem.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par For PDE verification, the differential equation and every boundary/initial condition must be checked separately.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-077-S-01} — Standard}
\begin{problembox}
A finite-difference model for electric/gravitational fields, gauss's law, charge and heat flux has one interior grid value $u$ surrounded by boundary values 12, 21, 32, and 41. For the five-point Laplace stencil, compute the interior value.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What PDE/BVP/field equation is being modeled?
\item What boundary and initial conditions are required?
\item What quantity is conserved or balanced?
\item Which part is analytical and which part requires discretization?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par The discrete Laplace equation sets the center equal to the average of its four nearest neighbors.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $u$ is the unknown interior grid value.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $-4u+u_E+u_W+u_N+u_S=0$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use a finite-difference discretization of the Laplace operator.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $u=(u_E+u_W+u_N+u_S)/4$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $u=(12+21+32+41)/4=26.500$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par A discrete harmonic interior value lies between the minimum and maximum surrounding boundary values; it does.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The interior grid value is $u=26.500$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par Discretization converts a differential field equation into an algebraic balance equation.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-077-A-01} — Advanced}

\textbf{Validate the result}\par For this quadratic exact solution, the centered second difference reproduces the second derivative exactly.\par\medskip

\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What PDE/BVP/field equation is being modeled?
\item What boundary and initial conditions are required?
\item What quantity is conserved or balanced?
\item Which part is analytical and which part requires discretization?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Discretize a two-point boundary-value problem into a small linear system.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $u_1\approx u(1/3)$ and $u_2\approx u(2/3)$; $h=1/3$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $-(u_{i-1}-2u_i+u_{i+1})/h^2=1$, hence $2u_i-u_{i-1}-u_{i+1}=h^2$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use centered finite differences with the boundary values inserted into the endpoint-adjacent equations.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $2u_1-u_2=h^2$ and $-u_1+2u_2=h^2$. Symmetry gives $u_1=u_2=h^2$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $u_1=u_2=1/9\approx 0.111111$. Exact $u(1/3)=u(2/3)=1/9\approx 0.111111$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par For this quadratic exact solution, the centered second difference reproduces the second derivative exactly.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par $u_1=u_2\approx 0.111111$, matching the exact values.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par A boundary-value differential equation becomes a sparse linear algebra problem after discretization.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\begin{reflectionbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item Which sentence or datum in these problems supplied the main mathematical constraint?
\item Which assumptions were essential to the model, and how would the model change if one were removed?
\item When would an analytical solution become inconvenient enough to justify a numerical method?
\end{enumerate}\end{reflectionbox}

\section*{Modeling Questions to Ask}\begin{itemize}
\item What PDE/BVP/field equation is being modeled?
\item What boundary and initial conditions are required?
\item What quantity is conserved or balanced?
\item Which part is analytical and which part requires discretization?
\end{itemize}
\begin{errorbox}Separate the mathematical model, boundary/initial conditions, and discretization. A numerically solvable scheme is not automatically stable or convergent.\end{errorbox}
\section*{Problems}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-081-F-01} — Foundation}
\begin{problembox}
For an advanced model related to architecture, bridge/building design and general engineering geometry, verify that $u(x,t)=e^{-\pi^2t}\sin(\pi x)$ satisfies the heat equation $u_t=u_{xx}$ and the boundary conditions $u(0,t)=u(1,t)=0$.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What PDE/BVP/field equation is being modeled?
\item What boundary and initial conditions are required?
\item What quantity is conserved or balanced?
\item Which part is analytical and which part requires discretization?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Verification means differentiate the candidate and check both the PDE and boundary data.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $u$ is a scalar field on $0\le x\le1$, $t\ge0$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par Heat equation: $u_t=u_{xx}$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use partial differentiation and direct substitution.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $u_t=-\pi^2 e^{-\pi^2t}\sin(\pi x)$ and $u_{xx}=-\pi^2 e^{-\pi^2t}\sin(\pi x)$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $u(0,t)=0$ and $u(1,t)=e^{-\pi^2t}\sin\pi=0$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The PDE identity and both boundary conditions hold for all $t\ge0$.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The candidate is a valid solution of the stated heat-equation boundary-value problem.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par For PDE verification, the differential equation and every boundary/initial condition must be checked separately.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection*{\pid{APP-081-S-01} — Standard}
\begin{problembox}
A finite-difference model for architecture, bridge/building design and general engineering geometry has one interior grid value $u$ surrounded by boundary values 11, 21, 30, and 41. For the five-point Laplace stencil, compute the interior value.
\end{problembox}
\begin{promptbox}\begin{enumerate}
\item What PDE/BVP/field equation is being modeled?
\item What boundary and initial conditions are required?
\item What quantity is conserved or balanced?
\item Which part is analytical and which part requires discretization?
\end{enumerate}\end{promptbox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par The discrete Laplace equation sets the center equal to the average of its four nearest neighbors.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $u$ is the unknown interior grid value.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $-4u+u_E+u_W+u_N+u_S=0$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use a finite-difference discretization of the Laplace operator.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $u=(u_E+u_W+u_N+u_S)/4$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $u=(11+21+30+41)/4=25.750$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par A discrete harmonic interior value lies between the minimum and maximum surrounding boundary values; it does.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The interior grid value is $u=25.750$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par Discretization converts a differential field equation into an algebraic balance equation.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}

\part{Mixed Application Review}
\chapter{Mixed Review — Technique Not Given}
The following problems intentionally omit the technique label. Decide how to model each problem before calculating.
\section*{Mixed Review 1: \pid{MR-01}}
\begin{problembox}For a short planning horizon, a quantity associated with population, cell-phone subscribers, gdp, apartment rental and other baseline trend models is modeled by $Q(t)=53e^{0.045t}$, where $t$ is measured in years. Find the instantaneous growth rate at $t=5$ and interpret it.\end{problembox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par The model is given; the requested quantity is the instantaneous rate of change.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $Q(t)$ is the modeled quantity and $t$ is time in years.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $Q(t)=53e^{0.045t}$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Differentiate because an instantaneous rate is requested.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $Q'(t)=53(0.045)e^{0.045t}=2.385e^{0.045t}$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $Q'(5)\approx 2.987$ units/year.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The derivative is positive, consistent with an increasing exponential model.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par At $t=5$, the quantity is increasing at approximately $2.987$ units per year.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The wording “instantaneous growth rate” identifies the derivative as the required operation.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\section*{Mixed Review 2: \pid{MR-02}}
\begin{problembox}A rigid segment of length 10 m is used in a angular speed and angular rate of change model. One endpoint moves horizontally away from a reference point at 1.00 m/s while the other endpoint moves vertically. When the horizontal distance is 6 m, how fast is the vertical distance changing?\end{problembox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par A fixed-length geometric constraint links two time-varying distances.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $x(t)$ is horizontal distance and $y(t)$ vertical distance.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $x^2+y^2=10^2$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use implicit differentiation with respect to time.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $2x\dot x+2y\dot y=0$, so $\dot y=-x\dot x/y$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $y=\sqrt{10^2-6^2}=8.000$ and $\dot y\approx -0.750$ m/s.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The negative sign means the vertical distance decreases while the horizontal distance grows.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The vertical distance is decreasing at approximately $0.750$ m/s.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The fixed length supplies the constraint; differentiating that constraint links the two rates.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\section*{Mixed Review 3: \pid{MR-03}}
\begin{problembox}A calibration equation for break-even point and cost–revenue intersections is $e^{-x}=x/2.0$. Use bisection on $[0,2]$ until the interval width is below $10^{-4}$, and report the solution.\end{problembox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par A transcendental equation must be solved numerically.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $f(x)=e^{-x}-x/2.0$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par The desired state satisfies $f(x)=0$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use bisection because $f$ is continuous and the endpoint signs differ.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $f(0)=1>0$ and $f(2)=-0.8647<0$. Repeated midpoint selection preserves the sign-changing bracket.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par Bisection gives $x\approx 0.85261$ with a final bracket narrower than $10^{-4}$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par $|f(0.85261)|\approx 6.66e-16$.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The calibrated value is approximately $x=0.85261$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The continuity and sign change justify bisection independently of derivative information.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\section*{Mixed Review 4: \pid{MR-04}}
\begin{problembox}A cost model for beam strength, illumination, engine efficiency and mechanical design is $C(x)=3100/x+84+0.6x$ for $x>0$. Find the operating value $x$ that minimizes cost and the minimum cost.\end{problembox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Minimize a positive-domain cost function.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $x>0$ is the decision variable and $C(x)$ the cost.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $C(x)=3100x^{-1}+84+0.6x$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Set the derivative equal to zero and verify convexity.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $C'(x)=-3100/x^2+0.6=0$, so $x=\sqrt{3100/0.6}$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $x\approx 71.880$ and $C(x)\approx 170.255$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par $C''(x)=2(3100)/x^3>0$ for $x>0$, so this is the unique minimum.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The minimum occurs at $x\approx 71.880$, with minimum cost approximately $170.255$ units.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par Convexity turns a stationary point into a verified global minimum on the positive domain.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\section*{Mixed Review 5: \pid{MR-05}}
\begin{problembox}In a arc length, cable length, gateway arch, pursuit and stream length application, liquid fills a vertical prismatic tank of height 6 m and horizontal cross-sectional area 4 m$^2$. Use a weight density of 9.8 kN/m$^3$. Find the work required to pump all liquid just to the top edge.\end{problembox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Each horizontal slice has a different lifting distance.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par Let $y$ be height above the tank bottom; a slice of thickness $dy$ has volume $A\,dy$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $dW=(9.8)(4)(6-y)\,dy$ kN·m.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Integrate slice work from bottom to top.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $W=9.8(4)\int_0^{6}(6-y)\,dy=9.8(4)(6^2/2)$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $W\approx 705.600$ kJ.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The bottom slices travel farthest and the top slice travels essentially zero distance; the integral reflects this.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par Approximately $705.600$ kJ of work is required.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The difficult step is modeling the differential work of a thin slice, not evaluating the integral.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\section*{Mixed Review 6: \pid{MR-06}}
\begin{problembox}In a probability integrals and expectation-like calculations application, liquid fills a vertical prismatic tank of height 5 m and horizontal cross-sectional area 2 m$^2$. Use a weight density of 9.8 kN/m$^3$. Find the work required to pump all liquid just to the top edge.\end{problembox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Each horizontal slice has a different lifting distance.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par Let $y$ be height above the tank bottom; a slice of thickness $dy$ has volume $A\,dy$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $dW=(9.8)(2)(5-y)\,dy$ kN·m.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Integrate slice work from bottom to top.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $W=9.8(2)\int_0^{5}(5-y)\,dy=9.8(2)(5^2/2)$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $W\approx 245.000$ kJ.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The bottom slices travel farthest and the top slice travels essentially zero distance; the integral reflects this.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par Approximately $245.000$ kJ of work is required.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The difficult step is modeling the differential work of a thin slice, not evaluating the integral.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\section*{Mixed Review 7: \pid{MR-07}}
\begin{problembox}A repeated contribution in special-function physics: pendulum refinements, elliptic geometry and spherical harmonics has magnitudes $315, 204.8, 133.1,\ldots$, with common ratio $r=0.65$. Determine the infinite accumulated total and state the convergence condition used.\end{problembox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par The repeated contributions form a geometric series.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $a=315$ and $r=0.65$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $S=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}ar^n$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use the geometric-series sum because $|r|<1$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $S=a/(1-r)$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $S=315/(1-0.65)=900.000$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par $|r|<1$, so the infinite series converges.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The total accumulated contribution is $900.000$ units.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The convergence test must be checked before applying the geometric-series formula.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\section*{Mixed Review 8: \pid{MR-08}}
\begin{problembox}A scalar field used in mass, moments and density distributions in two/three dimensions is $F(x,y,z)=x^2+2y^2+3z$. At $P=(3,2,1)$, find the gradient and the directional derivative toward the direction $(1,2,1)$.\end{problembox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par The gradient describes the direction of steepest increase; its dot product with a unit direction gives the directional derivative.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $F:\mathbb R^3\to\mathbb R$ and $\mathbf u=(1,2,1)/\sqrt6$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $\nabla F=(2x,4y,3)$ and $D_{\mathbf u}F=\nabla F\cdot\mathbf u$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use partial derivatives followed by a normalized-direction dot product.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $\nabla F(P)=(6,8,3)$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $D_{\mathbf u}F(P)=(6+2(8)+3)/\sqrt6\approx 10.2062$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The direction vector is normalized: $\|(1,2,1)/\sqrt6\|=1$.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par $\nabla F(P)=(6,8,3)$ and $D_{\mathbf u}F(P)\approx 10.2062$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par A directional derivative requires a unit vector; using an unnormalized direction would scale the rate incorrectly.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\section*{Mixed Review 9: \pid{MR-09}}
\begin{problembox}A bounded-growth model for torricelli flow, emptying tanks and hydraulic transients satisfies $y'=0.30y(1-y/540)$ with $y(0)=45$. Use the logistic solution to estimate $y(8)$.\end{problembox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Growth slows as the state approaches a carrying capacity.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $K=540$ is carrying capacity, $r=0.30$ is intrinsic rate, and $y_0=45$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $y(t)=K/(1+A e^{-rt})$ with $A=(K-y_0)/y_0$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use the separable logistic ODE solution and the initial condition.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $A=(540-45)/45=11.00000$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $y(8)\approx 270.284$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par $0<y(8)<540$, consistent with a logistic state below carrying capacity.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The model predicts $y(8)\approx 270.284$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par The carrying capacity changes the long-run behavior from unbounded exponential growth to saturation.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\section*{Mixed Review 10: \pid{MR-10}}
\begin{problembox}A small dataset for sales, inflation, health-care expenditures, government expenditures and gdp data has points $(0,6),(1,8),(2,10),(3,13)$. Find the least-squares line $y=mx+b$.\end{problembox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Fit a line that minimizes the sum of squared vertical residuals.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $m$ is slope and $b$ intercept.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $m=S_{xy}/S_{xx}$ and $b=\bar y-m\bar x$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Least squares can be derived by minimizing a quadratic error function; use its normal-equation formulas here.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $\bar x=1.50$, $\bar y=9.25$, $S_{xx}=5.00$, $S_{xy}=11.50$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $m=2.3000$, $b=5.8000$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par Residuals should sum to zero when an intercept is included; this fitted line satisfies that property up to rounding.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par $\hat y=2.3000x+5.8000$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par Regression chooses parameters by an optimization criterion rather than forcing the line through every noisy observation.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\section*{Mixed Review 11: \pid{MR-11}}
\begin{problembox}A four-sample signal in periodic temperature and respiratory-cycle signals is $x=[1,0,-1,0]$. Compute its unnormalized discrete Fourier transform $X_k=\sum_{n=0}^3 x_ne^{-2\pi i kn/4}$.\end{problembox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par Transform four equally spaced samples from the time/sample domain to discrete frequency coefficients.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $N=4$ and $X_k=\sum_{n=0}^{3}x_n e^{-2\pi i kn/4}$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par Only $x_0=1$ and $x_2=-1$ are nonzero, so $X_k=1-e^{-\pi i k}=1-(-1)^k$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use the DFT definition, the finite-dimensional basis of FFT algorithms.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par For even $k$, $X_k=0$; for odd $k$, $X_k=2$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $X=[0,2,0,2]$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par The transform is conjugate-symmetric because the input data are real.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The unnormalized DFT coefficients are $X_0=0,X_1=2,X_2=0,X_3=2$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par Sparse time-domain structure can yield a simple algebraic expression for every Fourier coefficient.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\section*{Mixed Review 12: \pid{MR-12}}
\begin{problembox}A finite-difference model for beam deflection, suspension bridges and boundary-constrained structural curves has one interior grid value $u$ surrounded by boundary values 13, 22, 30, and 40. For the five-point Laplace stencil, compute the interior value.\end{problembox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Understand the problem}\par The discrete Laplace equation sets the center equal to the average of its four nearest neighbors.\par\medskip
\textbf{Define variables}\par $u$ is the unknown interior grid value.\par\medskip
\textbf{Construct the model}\par $-4u+u_E+u_W+u_N+u_S=0$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Choose the calculus method}\par Use a finite-difference discretization of the Laplace operator.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solve symbolically}\par $u=(u_E+u_W+u_N+u_S)/4$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Substitute numerical data}\par $u=(13+22+30+40)/4=26.250$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validate the result}\par A discrete harmonic interior value lies between the minimum and maximum surrounding boundary values; it does.\par\medskip
\textbf{State the final answer}\par The interior grid value is $u=26.250$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Solution insight}\par Discretization converts a differential field equation into an algebraic balance equation.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\part{Capstone Modeling Problems}
\chapter{Interdisciplinary Capstones}
\section*{\pid{CAP-01} — Solar-storage design}
\begin{problembox}A cylindrical thermal-storage tank must hold 120 m$^3$. The lateral material costs twice as much per square meter as the top and bottom material. Model total relative material cost, determine the radius and height minimizing it, and then estimate how a 1\% radius fabrication error changes the optimized volume using differentials.\end{problembox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Model}\par $V=\pi r^2h=120$, so $h=120/(\pi r^2)$. Relative cost is $C=4\pi rh+2\pi r^2=480/r+2\pi r^2$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Optimize}\par $C'(r)=-480/r^2+4\pi r=0$ gives $r^3=120/\pi$, so $r\approx 3.368$ m and $h\approx 3.368$ m.\par\medskip
\textbf{Sensitivity}\par $dV/V\approx2\,dr/r+dh/h$. If only radius has a 1\% error while height is fixed, $dV/V\approx2\%$, or about $2.400$ m$^3$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validation}\par $C''(r)=960/r^3+4\pi>0$, so the stationary design is a minimum.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\section*{\pid{CAP-02} — Tracking and interception}
\begin{problembox}A sensor reports a target position $\mathbf r(t)=(20+4t,\,10+2t,\,100-5t^2)$ meters. Determine velocity and acceleration, find the time when altitude reaches 40 m, and compute the horizontal distance traveled by then.\end{problembox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Kinematics}\par $\mathbf v=(4,2,-10t)$ and $\mathbf a=(0,0,-10)$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Altitude event}\par $100-5t^2=40$ gives $t=\sqrt{12}\approx 3.464$ s.\par\medskip
\textbf{Horizontal distance}\par The horizontal displacement is $(4t,2t)$, so its magnitude is $t\sqrt{20}\approx 15.492$ m.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validation}\par The positive time root is used because time is nonnegative.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\section*{\pid{CAP-03} — Heat-and-data model}
\begin{problembox}Temperature in a small test chamber follows $T'= -0.18(T-22)$ with $T(0)=90$. Find the exact model, determine when $T=35$, and use two Euler steps of size 1 minute to approximate the first two minutes for comparison.\end{problembox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Exact model}\par $T(t)=22+68e^{-0.18t}$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Threshold time}\par $35=22+68e^{-0.18t}$ gives $t\approx 9.192$ min.\par\medskip
\textbf{Euler comparison}\par $T_1=77.760$ and $T_2=67.723$ for $h=1$ min.\par\medskip
\textbf{Validation}\par Both exact and Euler trajectories move toward the ambient temperature 22°C.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}
\section*{\pid{CAP-04} — Field-flux calculation}
\begin{problembox}A vector field is $\mathbf F=(x,y,z)$ and $S$ is the sphere of radius 2. Use the divergence theorem to compute outward flux and interpret the result as total source strength inside the sphere.\end{problembox}
\begin{solutionbox}
\textbf{Model}\par $\nabla\cdot\mathbf F=1+1+1=3$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Divergence theorem}\par $\iint_S\mathbf F\cdot\mathbf n\,dS=\iiint_V3\,dV=3\cdot\frac43\pi(2^3)=32\pi$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Numerical value}\par $32\pi\approx 100.531$.\par\medskip
\textbf{Interpretation}\par Positive outward flux indicates a net source distributed throughout the enclosed volume.\par\medskip
\end{solutionbox}

\chapter{Sources and Scope Notes}
\begin{itemize}
\item The application inventory and calculus-topic crosswalk are derived from the supplied calculus source and its application index; problems in this workbook are newly authored.
\item The modeling pedagogy is an original synthesis of Eugene Don and Benay Don, \emph{How to Solve Word Problems in Calculus: A Solved Problem Approach} (McGraw-Hill, 2001), especially its emphasis on constructing a mathematical model before solving it and on learning general procedures rather than memorizing isolated templates.
\item Numerical extensions use the method taxonomy of Press, Teukolsky, Vetterling, and Flannery, \emph{Numerical Recipes: The Art of Scientific Computing}, Third Edition (Cambridge University Press, 2007).
\item Proof-oriented explanations use the writing and proof-strategy principles of Chartrand, Polimeni, and Zhang, \emph{Mathematical Proofs: A Transition to Advanced Mathematics}, Fourth Edition, only where justification improves understanding.
\end{itemize}
